I. Indefinite Continuation of the Occupation
Under this course the United States would publicly announce
that it planned to remain in occupation in Japan until Japanese
security could be effectively ensured, by the UN or by other
meens, without our presence. A drastic reorganization of
the occupation, along the lines of NSC 13/3 but cerried further,
would be instituted in light of this decision. Necessary U.S.
economic eid vould be continued. Expension of Japanese foreign
trade, includine trede with Communist-dominated areas subject to
minimum security safeguarus, would be encoureged and assisted.
When it beceme apparent thst continuation of direct U.S. control
in Japan was no longer necessary--effective UN security forces
having been established, Jrpen being firmly "on our side", or
the U.S.-UssR strugle having sufficiently eesed--we would
egsin consider the desirebility of e pesce settlement.

4. Increasing dissatiefaction with our failure to proceed
with a treaty and continued virtually exclusive control in Japan
could be expected from at least some of our Allies.
Recent months have witnessed renewed interest in a peace
treaty not only by the Soviets but also by the British, Australians
and other of cur Allies, who have shown increasing dissatisfaction
with what they regard as U.S. unilateralism under the present
regime of control for Japan. Perpetuation of this regime throughout
an indefinitely continued occupation would probably be opposed by
our Allies, but no plan for its alteration could be expected to
win majority acceptance including the four veto powers. Disappear-

II. Conclusion of a Peace Settlement
Under this course the United States would undertake to
conclude a peace treaty with Japan oarticipated in by as many
nations at war with Japan es would accept our minimum desiderata.
The United States would first discuss the content and
procedures of a treaty with the Governments of the ten other
FEC nations, explaining our position on the major issues in a
manner and to a degree appropriate to each nation. If assurances
vere forthcoming from at lesst six of these nations not necessarily
including the Soviet Union and China) that they would sign a peace
treaty which we felt embodied our minimum desiderata, we would
convene a conference of these FEC nations to draft the treaty,
which would subsequently be submitted to the other nations at
war with Japan. The U.S. would decide before convening the
conference

requisite essurances were not forthcoming from at least six FEC

to it of as many countries at war with Japan as possible.
Our position on the major points of content in a treaty
during the diplomatic discussions with the FEC nations and other
nations wo ld be (1) that the treaty be brief and general;
(2) that it contain e statement of general principles, possibly.
patterned on the principles of the United Nations Charter, to

internattonal territorial settlements; (4) that it provide for
the settlement of outstanding property claims or for general
principles governing this subject, leaving details for later
individual negotiation; (5) that it record the fulfillment
by Japan of its industrial reparations obligations; (6) that
it obligate Japan to  rant all rights to foreign nationals
in Jepen reciprocally granted to Jepan by other states; (7)
that it not prohibit the establishment of defense forces by
Japan, nor restrict Japanese industriel capacity or production;
(8) that it not provide for any post-treaty controls in Japan;
(9) and that it not prohibit Japan from entering into bilateral o

1. Date and time: 11:00 a.m. 0 4:00 p.m., May 31.
2. Sponsor: Federation of Tokyo Municipal Workers'
Unions (TO-ROREN)
3. Objective: Demonstrating opposition to Public
Safety Ordinance Bill.
4. Attendants: About 10,000 workers.
5. Details of the meeting: While the meeting was
scheduled as stated above, about 2,ooo members of
Federation of Tokyo Municipal Workers' Unions (TO-RoREN),
All-Japan Newspapermen Union (ZEN-SHIMBUN), All-Japan
Metal Industry Workers' Union (ZEN-KINZOKU), All-Japan
Pressmen Union (ZEN-INSATSU), General Federation of
Private Railroad Woriers' Union (SHITETSU-SOREN), All
Japan Government and Munioipal Workers' Union (ZEN-KANKO),
and Eleotrio Industry Workers' Union (DEN-SAn), asscmhled
about noon and held a meeting in considerably high spirits
to demonstrate their opposition to Publio Safety Ordinance
Bill and about the poor HASHIMOTO, Kinj1, of Tokyo
Street-car Workers' Union (TOko) injured to death at the
meeting held on May 30, and the meeting adjourned at 2:25
p.m. Some of the attendants who refused to disperse rushed

FOREWORD
This report is an annlysis of the results
of two questions asked during a survey
conducted by the Jiji Nows Agency Public
Opinion Room. It is one of a s eries of
brief analyses of surveys conducted by
Japanese agencies of the attitudes of the
Japanese peopio tovard significent cur-
rent issues. Thoso analyses of surveys
conducted by Japanese public opinion
agencies do not constitute either an in-
dorsement of the agency or approval of
its findings.

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.98)
Basic Attitudes Toward Foreign Countrios
INTRODUCTION
During the first four days of May 1949, the Jiji News Agency Public
Opinion Room -- during the course of a survey on tax problems - asked a
nationwide sample of 8,Oll adults two direct questions: "What foreign
country do you like the most?" and "What foreign country do you dislike the
most?" This direct technique, although incapable of revealing nuances of
meaning, or of examining the assumptions upon which people made their se
lections, nevertheless did reveal a polarization of attitudes toward the
United States and the Soviet Union whiah is considered to be of considerable
significance.
Several limitations of the survey should be noted. First, the proper
interpretation of "like? was left up to the respondent. He could construe
it to mean "I would like to live in that country, " "I think that country has
a good government, a fine way of life, an ideologically sound political
organizetion, or a commendable forcign policy, " "I liko the people and the
culturo of that country," etc. Similar intorprotations could be mado for
"dislike" as woll.
Secondly, respondents were not offercd the opportunity to statc second
choices. If this had boen done, a. moro accurato interpretation might have
been made of some of the first choices. Third, the facts that the survey
was conducted in a country under American cccupation, and that outstanding
and fundamental differences in viowpoints oxist betwoon the U.S. and the
U.S.S.R. undoubtedly had some influonco over the selection of favored and
nonfavorod foreign countries. Somo persons who likod Russia might have
hositated to say so oponly to an intorviewer, and the same (or other) per-
sons might similarly have reen disinclinod to state a dislike for the United
States. However, even if (as noted bolow) tho porcontages liking America
and disliking Russia are taken as maximum and minimum figures, respcctively,
they indicate a very substantial amount of favorablo public opinion toward
America, and, indirectly, theyprovido a usoful index of the success of tho
Occupation in fostcring an interost in and an approval of democracy and the
domocratic way of life.
1. Seo APPENuIx for an uxpl n tien or t! is nunb r nu  brief lescription
of tho sampling method used.
2. Suki -- to liko
3. Kirau -- to dislike

A majority of the Japancso poople

liked or disliked by rolatively small porcentages.
It should bo noted that because of the dynamics of the testing situation
(sec Introduction), the porcentages favoring America and disliking Russia
are probably maximum figures, whilo the percentages disliking Amcrica and
favoring Russia are pr bably minimum figures. Furthermore, some of tho
relatively large mumber of peoplc who stated that they disliked no country
(34 percent) might have given this answer because of a disinclination to
state a dislike for America.

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.98)
ATTENDIX
The Survey Mothod
A total of 5,998 respondents were interviewed during the period
1-4 May 1949. The sample was dravn from those porsons in the Japanese
population over 20 years of age, and is a cross section of that group.
Since rural areas are so large, adequate sampling can be obtained by
using fewer cases than is possible in urban areas. Therefore, the ratios
used wero one respcndent to every 5,000 persons of this age group in citiea
and one responient to every 10,000 persons in rural areas. The number of
interviows mado in rural areas was not in proportion to tho population of
Japan, so a standard statistical weighting system was usod. The woighted
sample numbered 8,0ll, nd ali rofernces to "nuabor of respondents" in
the text of this analysis refer to the weighted sample.
The sample wes construeted by using the "1948 Ration Ledger Census
of Nonmigrant Persons" as a baso. The area-random-sampling-method was
utilized in selocting respondents. A total of 3l6 sample points was used:
124 cities (including Tokyo), 86 tovms, and 106 villages.

National Council of Labor Lnions.
The National Council of Labor tnions (Zenro Kaigi)
was organized by the leaders of Sanbetsu Mindo, Kokutetsu
Mindo and the left-wing leaders of the Sodomei. Leaders
of independent national unions have expressed an interest

ZenroKaigi is comparable to the National Liaison

national unions such as Kokutetsu. reniing the organization
of the Mindo unions into a federation which will be

will oppose any attempts by the Socialists to dominate
the Council. Although the organization of Zenro Kaigi
is still in a formative stage, it is a potential field
of conflict between the Sodomei and the Mindo federation
on the one hand, and between the leaders of these two
federations and the Socialist Party on the other.
The Mindo unions will te organized into an industrial
labor federation whose leaders will be affiliated with
the Socialist Party. Within the party Mindo will be
associated with left-wing party leaders which will strengthen
this group vis-a-vis the right wing of the party. Sodomei
is also an industrial labor federation with close tles to
the Socialist Farty but Sodomei is divided between right
and left wing factions of which the right wing appears.
to be stronger. To date the leaders of Mindo and Sodomei
have cooperated with each other and Mindo has refrained
from working within Sodomei unions. In the formation of
Zenro Kaigi the two federations will be in direct competition
for Teadership of the Council and this competition will.
be accentuated if Sodomei is controlled by its right wing.
If the right wing in Sodomei succeeds in dominating that
federation it is possible that not only the Sodomei but
also the Socialist Party will split and the left wing of
both party and federation would join with Mindo to form
a new political group. Lindo leaders estimate now that
this situation will not come to a head for about a year.
Meanwhile, Mindo, working with the left-wing of the Sodomei,
will attempt to reconstruct the Socialist Party as a
political instrument of. the non-communist. labor movement
using the same tactics of working from within therparty
organization as they are"now using within the labd unions.

Tokyo's Despatch no. 549,

-2-
The rise of the Yoshida Faction, concomitant with
the aecline of the other party factions, is tending
to . attention to the problem of Mr. Yoshida's
eventual successor as head of the Democratic Liberal
Party. In replacing old-line politicians, ome of whom
have had potentialities for party leadership, Mr. Yoshida
has brought into the party large numbers of new men many
of whom at present lack sufficient political experience
to qualify for effective party leadership. This situation
might lead to difficult complioations if Mr. Toshida were
forced to withdraw for health or other reasons. In view.
of the Prime Minister's advanced age and several recent
illnesses such possibilities are creating some concern
and speculation in political circles.

MEMORANDUM
June 15, 1949.
Subject: Factions in the Demooratio Liberal Party
Attached is a list of Democratic Party (DLP members
of the House of Representatives indicating affiliation
with the intra-party factions as of May 1, 1949 and previous
factional affiliations in cases where shifts have been
made since March 31, 1949. This list was oompiled by a
reliable Japanese source close to the Demooratic Liberal
Party. It should be borne in mind, however, that any
listing of fcctional affiliations is subject to some.
inaccuracies arising out of the intangible oharaoter of
factional affiliations as contrasted to party membership.
This list of the members of the various intra-party
factions in the DIP indicates. two significant develop-
ments. First, Prime Minister YOSHIDA Shigeru is continuing
to strengthen his position within the party despite the
fact that he has been forced to compromise the party's
campaign promises in formulating a balanoed budget.
Secondly, the power of the politioal bosses in the party
is rapidiy dwindling to insignifioant oppositions. There
remain within the party dissident factions opposed for
various reaons to Yoshida, but these opposition factions
are losing adherents and their positions have been con-
siderably weakened.
This situation is further borne out by the feot that
opposition within the DLP to the inolusion of Mr: IUKAI
Ken and his Demooratic Party faotion in the DLP has been
defeated and there appeurs to be little possibility that
the inolusion of Inukai in the DLP will have any serious
consequenoes on party unity. Yoshida, who has consistently
supported the Inukai merger against strong opposition
within his own party, hes finally won out. Furthermore,
he has suoceeded in obtaining his party's approval to the
proposal that the Mshida faotion of the Demooratio Party
be invited to join the DLP. This invitation was e: nded
not to Mr. Ashida, who is temporarily inaotive pending
the sottlement of his indiotment, but to Mr. TOMABECHI
Gizo the present leader of the Ashida faotion.
The inoret ing strength of Yoshida within the DLP
in turn raises two problems of oonsiderable importance
to the party in the future. Will Yoshida sucoeed in
developing new political leaders from among the ranks
of his party, and secondly, who will succeed Yoshida
as president of the party? Yoshida has been foroed to
gather around him men who re relatively new to politios,
although these men in most istances have had oonsiderable
experience in government service or in business. In this
sense Yoshida is bringing to the fore men new to party
politios to replace the longtime politicians who have
attempted
CONF IDENTIAL

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.99)
Enolosure to Tokyo's
549, August 11, 1949
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
attempted to oontinue their hold on the party either
directly or through their proteges. The problem of who
will succeed Yoshida as leader of the party, especially
if in the near future he should be unable to continuo
actively in the party, is more than a question of the
development of new leadership. Although there is within
the party a number of able men who are potential party
leaders, there appears at this time to be no suitable
candidate who could qualify in terms of political
experience or ability and who at the same time has
sufficient political power to maintain party unity.
Although Yoshide has succeeded in dominating the party
and reducing the power of the opposition factions, his
removal from the leadership of the party would be a
signal for the recons titution of many of the factional
groups that are now relatively impotent. If, on the
other hand, Yoshida continues as party leader for two
or three years he would probably be able to develop a
core of new experienced leadership capable of taking
over the party.
The Yoshida Faction
At the present time this faction consists of about
eighty-six members, as compared to twenty members prior
to March 31, 1949. In addition to this core of close
and fairly reliable supporters, Yoshida still draws
considerable support from the so-called neutral members
of the party, who remain outside factional groups primarily
because they a re new to party politios. The new members
of the Yoshida group ha ve ocme from all of the other
factions, but primarily from the neutral group and from
the Ono and Shidehara factions.
The adherenoe of former neutrals to the Yoshida
Faction is not in itself of any special signifioance.
These members were either newly elected, and therefore
potentially Yoshida men, or were second or third term
party members who were not neoessarily opposed to
Yoshida but were merely unwilling to commit themselves
in terme of intra-party groups.
The addition of members of the Ono faotion to
Yoshida's group is, on the other hand, a reflection of
the deoline in power of the oldline party bosses, and
indireotly of HAToYAiA Iohiro, the purged former president
of the party, and represents a strengthening of Yoshida's
party position that is out of proportion to the members
of Ono men who have joined his group. Nineteen former
members of the Ono faotion are now considered to belong
to Yoshida's group. Ono's connection with Hatoyama and
other oldtime party leaders has been the source of his
power within the DLP, as well as the faot that Yoshida
in the early days of his party leadership needed the
support of experienced politicians. Ono's indictment
and
CONFIDENTIAL

-3-
and recent sentence to ten months in prison for accepting
bribes has been an immediate cause for his deoline in
power within the party. Yoshida was at least relieved
to have Ono out of a position of influence within the
party, and while there is no evidence, despite the many
rumors,tt t Yoshida exerted any pressure to bring about
Ono's conviction, he did nothing to assist Ono in his
trial.
The Ono Faction
The Ono faction has been the stronghold.of the
influence and power of prewar political leaders within
the DIP. It was, prior to its recent deoline, composed
of oldtime political leaders still in public life, along
with a few younger members who looked to the older
politicians as their mentors. This group has been
opposed to Yoshida as an interloper who instead of
considering himself merely the temporary custodian of
Hatoyama's power began to build up his own political
power independent of Hatoyama, and gradually freed himself
of dependence upon the Ono faction for support and advice.
It was this group that strongly opposed the ooncessions
Yoshida made in the formulation of the recent budget,
the coalition cabinet with the Demoorats, and the proposal
that Inukai enter the DLP.
At the present the Ono faction consists of about
ulirty-rlye er bore  ompared to forty-eight at the
end ofmyaroh: although the deoline in terms of numbers
is not large, the grcup has been deprived of Ono's
leadership and. Yoshida has removed most of its members
from positions of party responsibility thereby depriving
them of their ability to control the polioies of the
party, whioh in turn weakens their position to attraot
new Diet and party members to their group.
The Yamazaki Faotion
This group has any of the ohardoteristios of the
Ono fuotion and is in fuot fairly olose to the latter
group on mattere of purty polioy. Yumazaki and Yoshida
have been on very bad terms sinoe the attempt last.
fall by dissidonts within the DLP and elements with in
the Demooratio Party to nominate Yamazaki instead of
Yoshida as Premier. Although Yamazaki resigned from the
Diet to avoid being a oandidate against Yoshida and
stated publioally thet he was an innooent viotim of. the
maohinations of other groups, Yoshida has been unfriendly
toward him cvor since. Yamazaki's faotion has gained
four members sinoe Maroh, but he has lost the support
of Mr. HOSHIJIMA Jiro, who, although still considered
a member of this faotion, is leaning more and more toward
Yoshida.
The
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
Tra Shidehara Faction
This faction consisted of those former Demoorats
who joined the DIP in March 1948 after seceding from
the Democratio Party over such issues as the coal bill
and the formation of the Ashida Cabinet. The faction
was primarily interested in assuring the former Demoorats
a position of influence in the DIP. With the elevation
of Shidehara to the speakership of the House of Repre-
sentatives, and with the gradual assimilation of the
members of this faotion into the DLP, the Shidehara
faction is declining in importance and its members are
drawing closer to Yoshida.
The Shukuto Faction
The Shukuto, or Party Cleaning Faction, presently
consists of thirteen members as opposed to twenty-two
members earlier this year.It is composed largely of
younger Diet members who desire to reform the party
by eliminating the control of the older politioians and
giving the younger members an opportunity to hold
influential positions. This group has been in opposition
to Yoshida and has čonstituted a dissident element
within the party on almost every issue. The decline of
this group and the defection of some of its members to
the Yoshida, Yamazaki and Ono factions is indicative
of the mixed composition of the original group and also
of its failure to attain any influence within the party.
The Neutral Group
This group is primarily composed of the newer Diet
members who have not formed olose politioal friendships
and are generally inexperienced in party politios. Since
the general eleotion of January 23, 1949, the group has
been reduced by some thirty-three members most of whom
joined the Yoshida faotion. In intra-party affairs the
neutral group is not oonsidered a faction. The name is
merely a conveinient designation for all party members
who are not affiliated with the existing faotions. The
various faotions seek the support of these neutral
members to strengthen their intra-party positions, but
the majority of the neutrals usually support the dominant
group within the party, whioh is now the Voshida faction.
Yoshida's Negotiations with the Demoorats
Yoshida 1s continuing to strengthen his position
within theparty by drawing into its ranks themembers
of both fuotions of the Demooratic Party." Initially
he insisted on the formation of a coalition cabinst
with Inukai and his Democratio party supporters. This
effort failed, and more recently Yoshida has succeeded
in winning the DLP over to accepting Inukai and his
followers into the DLF. Originally the Ono, Yamazaki
and Shukuto fuctions strongly opposed any merger with
the coalition Democrats and it apreared that if Yoshida
foroed the issue he might parcipitate a split in his

Yoshida is now trying to bring the opposition Demoorats

UNESCO

UNA

EUR

and has been undertaken in response to requests for

XIR
country. Heretofore, speakers have taken a tolerant
attitude toward the Communists, but no effort is now
being made to win over persons of communist sympathy,
894.00B
and speakers are desoribing with frankness the harsh
realities of communist regimes and the treacherous
taotios of international communism.
The Foreign Office official was impressed with the
faot that communist activity in Hokkaido appears weaker
than in other parts of Japan and that there is a definite
and conspicuous anti-communist tendency among the people.
One of the most active of ecently organized anti-communist
groups is the Heiwa Domei (Peace Alliance), which has
successfully penetrated Tabor unions in several coal
mines. In some instances, there is a tendency for the
Heiwa Domei to become affiliated with local UNEsco chapters.
Hokkaido Counter Intelligence Corps informed the
Japanese official that there are believed to be about
nine thousand communist party members on the island, along
with some thirty thousand communist sympathizers.
The official also found great enthusiasm for and
interest in the UNESco movement, the meetings of the
local UNEsco chapters being exceptionally well attended.

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatoh nov
dated August 27, 1949 from
the United States Politioal
Adviser for Japan, Tokyo,
on the subject, "Communist
Situation in Hokkaido
(COPY)
Received from K, Ohno, Japanese Foreign Office, August 19,
1949。
Recent Information Concerning Hokkaido
Observation Made by the Foreign Office Official
From 5th to l0th of this month, and official of
the Foreign Office made a tour of Hokkaido and spoke
before the Sapporo Gaiko-Kodankai (Sapnoro UNESco
Chapter, Sapporo Nittsu, Bibai UNESco, and before the
joint meeting sponsored by Hakodate Chamber of Commerce
and Industry, Hakodate Operators' Associacion and
Hakodate Shimbun. Following is the impression which
the lecturer acquired from his brief tour.
1. General Impression
In comparison with the communist activities in
Honshu and other parts of Japan, the leftist influence.
is not so strong in Hokkeido aná condition there is
far more stable then what is senerally felt in Tokyo.
Actually anti-communist movements are very conspicuous
and this tendency is daily increasing, particularly
since the establishment of Heiwa Domei (Peace Alliance)
which has penetrated into various coal mines. However,
there are rumours to the effect that this Alliance is
inclining towards the rightist ideology and may amalgamate
various UNEsco chapters (as in the case of Bibai).
According to the chief of Counter Intelligence Corps
in Hokkaido, Lt. Col. De Loach, "Communism will not
flourish where people enjoy three square meals a day."
This is true for the standard of living in Hokkaido is
much higher than in the main island of Japan. Colonel
De Loach also added that according to the rough estimation
by the Japanese police, there are roughly 9ooo communists
and 30,oo0 sympathizers in Hokkaido. Although communists
are rather weak in Hokkaido, they still maintain far
stronger organization than the Peace Alliance Group.
Cne ver distinct tendency in Hokkaido is that the
UNESCO movement is very popular there. For example a
very old man over 80 years of age regularly attends the
UNESCO

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo!s
590, August 27, 1949.
-2-
UNESCO gathering in Sapporo and a man over 60 is the
President of Bibai Chapter of UNESCo.  At Bibai young
men and women as well as mothers attend the meeting,
even carrying babies cn their backs.
The Hokkaido Liaison Office of the Foreign Office
is exerting its great efforts in fostering UNESCo
movement. Perhaps this is accomplished because of the
peoples responsi ve attitude to new things in Hokkaido.
2. Attitude of the People Attending Lecture Meetings.
Peoples in various places in Hokkaido are very
earnest and many questions were asked at the gatherings.
Also it could be said that people in Hokkaido are nowwell
acquainted with the communistic tactics in various labor
unions and they are aware of the dangerous objectives
of the Communist Party.
3. Further Desires for Hokkaido.
Although anti-communist measures are already set
up in Hokkaido, there is a possibility that the Communists
may recover their lost influence in that locality. As
a consequence, the laborers in coal mines especially were
very anxious to know about the actual conditions existing
in USSR.
The Foreign Office official spoke mainly on the
history, politics, economics and social condition of the
United States, but he was showered with questions concern-
ing USSR. One of the interpellators stated that they
are well informed on American affairs, but on Russian
affairs their knowledge is limited. It is felt therefore
that further lectures on Russian affairs are necessary
in Hokkaido area to consolidate the anti-communist
movement. The workers in Hokkaido pointed out that they
are fully aware of the American labor problems and they
are looking forward to having an opportunity of listening
to an expert on Russian labor problems.
4. Questions Asked by the Audience.
(a) Will America withdraw from Japan in event of war
with Russia? The comm nists are saying that in case of
shooting war between the Russians and Americans, the
Russian Army will land in Hokkaido and therefore it is
better to join the Japan Communist Party.
(b) Will the Japan Communist Party resort to
violence? The Japan Communist Party apparently says no,
but what is the truth?

JUME 1949 REPORT FROM
JAPAN
POLITICAL SITUATION:
Elections and Changes in Government
The Government has formally decided to appoint Mr. Kaneshichi Masuda,
present Chief Cabinet Secretary, to the State Minister at the Cabinet Meeting hold
on June 24. The inauguration ceremony was taken place on the same day.
The Ministry of Communications became divided into two Ministries
of Postal Administration and Telecommunications upon the principle of its
complete reorganization stated in the General Mao Arthur's letter dated on June
22nd, 1948 relative to the fundamental revision of National Publio Service Law,
for the purposenof more efficient and powerful management of its business --
the former administrates postal affairs, savings and insurance, the latter
administrates telegraph and telephone communications. These two Ministries
have started on June lst in accordance with the Establishment Law of Postal
Administration Ministry and Telecommunications Ministry.
Mr. Saeki Osawa, former Minister of Communications has been appointed
to head the both Ministries concurrently on June lst.
International relations, including particularly commercial
agreement and trade treaties
According to the telegram from Buenos Aires a new commercial finanoe
agreement was made on June 8, between Argentina Government and Japanese trade
mission while on their inspection trip to Central and South America. Although
dotails are not disclosed, commercial circles in Buenos Aires are said to be
making the following conjectures:
(1) Argentina will send to Japan cereals, raw wool and hides in
exchange for tea, silk, rice and machinery.
(2) Payments will be in U.S. dollars when the balance on one
side or the other reaches $ 3,000,000.
(3) Each country, under the agreement, is to use its own ships
as much as possible in the two-way trade.
It was announced on June 9th by the Public Relations Section, G.H.Q.
that a trade arrangement totalling  2,500,000 was signed between the French
Union and Occupied Japan. The arrangement will cover trade between the two
countries from March 1st, 1949 to Februery 28th, 1950 and will be conducted
in accordance with the terms of the financial arrangement in effect between
the French Union and Ocoupied Japan since July 7th, 1948.

JUNE 1949 REPORT FROM JAPAN
All matters pertaining to both the trade arrangement and the finas-
cial plan, have the approval bcth of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
and of the French Government.
It was announced formally by the Ministry of Finance of Peru that a
commercial arrangement was made on June 24th, in Lima, between Japanese trade
mission and Peru officials concerned.
The arrangement contains that the trade is based on dollar account
and Japan will import raw cotton, sugar and metals in exchange for ordinary
machines, steel products, paper, cement and fibre products not manufactured
in Peru.
It was announced by the Public Relations Section, G.H.Q. that a
commercial arrangement totalling $ 5 million was signed between Finland and
Japan on June 2lst.
The term of validity of this arrangement is one year from July lst
this year, and the American dollar settlement of open account system is adopted.
According to information from the Government circles of Pakistan
obtained on June l6th, a seven-point trade agreement was reached by Pakistan
and Japan and would be effective July lst.
Under its terms Japan will export cloth, cotton yarn, rayons, elect-
rical goods, glassware, iron products, clocks and watohes. Japan will receive
85,000 bales of ran jute, 20,000 tons of salt, 500,000 bales of wood,
1,500,000 pounds of hemp and other erticles.
Attitude of Government towards business
As the Ministry of Communications was divided into the Ministries of
Postal Administration and Telecommnications on June lst, and at the same time
the self-supporting accounting system has definitely been adopted by them, their
policy of purchasing from manufacturers would be much influonced.
The Communications Bureau of Economic Stabilisation Board was
abolished and the business belcnging to it has been succeeded by the Traffio
Section of Construction and Traffic Bureau of the Board.
Specific legislation introduced which may affect Companies
interests
The Law amending the Anti-Monopoly Law was promulgated on June 18th.
The salient points of the revision are as follows:

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
JUNE 1949 REPORT PROM JAPAN
(1)
Under the provisions of the original law, any international
agreements or contracts placing "restrictions on import of technique,
agreement on production and sales" by Japanese Companies were requir-
ed to be previously approved by the Fair Trade Commission, and the
"restrictions on the exchange of kmowledge or information regarding
science or technique" were also banned.
The revised law has removed these prohibitions of the restriot-
ions on the exchange or knowledge or information regarding science
or technique, changed the requirement of obtaining approval before-
hand to an ex post facto report on the international agreements or
contracts, ana prohibited only such ones as would violate the anti-
trust-and-cartel principle. (Article 6).
(2)
The original lew, as a principie, prohibited enterprises except
banking business to own ahares of other industries and so obstructed
the foreign countries of the revival of pre-war investment and their
new investments. The revision has allowed altogether the business
enterprises to own shares of other industries except when they are
competitive ones, restrict the transactions, and result in monopoly.
(Article 10).
(3)
The original law restricted the floatation of debentures ex-
pressed by foreign currency basing upon the restrictions on holding
debentures; whereas the revision deleted the clause which states that
no one can hold debentures of more than 25% of the capital of the
company. (Article 12).
(4)
The revised law has banned the concurrent assumption of
officlals only when the Companies are competitive. (Article 13).

Shimbun. Except for several communist leaders, Mr. Fuse

comment by Dr. B. NICOLAEVSKY in his article "Russia,

in the May 1949 issue of The Far Eastern Cuarterly.

prophetic of the present situation. In 1920 Lenin,
according to the author, declared that at that stage real
communism had little possibility of success except in
already digging their own future graves. Stalin in 1925

By Katsuji Fuse

Subject:
Filipino Attitude towards Post-war Japan as Exemplified
SEP 22
by Speech of Filipino Diplomat and Comment of Terspaper

The Honorable
FoS-ens
T.e Secretary of State,
ITP
Washington, D. C.
OFD
Sir :
1!
I have the honor to transmit herewith the published text of a
statement of September 1, 1949 made by Dr. Bernabe AFRICA, Philippine
Envoy accredited to SCAP, in regard to progress made in Japan in the
four years since that nation's surrender, As Dr. Africa formerly was
Undersecretary of Foreign Affairs his views are likely to carry a
measure of weight within the Philippine Government. Moreover, as the
statement of an informed Filipino official, his words may have
greater influence on Philippine attitudes vis-a-vis Japan than simi-
lar statements released by SCAP or by the United States Government.
In his statement, which is almost entirely laudatory of the
poli ies and accomplishments of SCAP and which evinces a sympathetic
attitude toward Japanese problems, Dr. frica makes statements about
Japan which include the following: Japan has been disarmed and de-
militarized, and those Japanese groups responsible for launching the
country on a career of aggression have been broken up and deprived of
power. Under SCAP's guidance, a democratic constitution has been
adopted and accompanying changes have been made in the country's po-
litical organization. The educational system has been reorganized
and its methods and content redirected along democratic lines. Shin-
toism has been disestablished as a state religion. Equality of the
sexes has become an established fact. Labor unions have been en-
C
ouraged and sweeping land-reforms have been accomplished. (Dr.
FILED
Africa's only unfavorable comment was an assertion that the United
States policy in regard to reparations, though understandable, is re-
grettable because it overlooks the interests of the countries which
suffered most and because it removes a deterrent to future aggressors
Dr. Africa also describes the problems of overpopulation and foodstuf
shortages, points out the related necessity for restoring industry and
FAP
foreign trade, and cites progress made in the direction of restoring
the national economy.
While the passage of time and helpful statements by Filipinos
like Dr. Africa may be expected eventually to soften Filipino atti-
udes toward Japan, it would be a mistake to assume that hatred of the
Japanese by the people of this country has yet been greatly diminished.
Indeed, conversation with numerous Filipinos leaves the impression
that it will be a long time before the people of this country forget
Tnige

the

not yet a safe place for Japanese.

This article speaks my common sense view without con-
sulting with any other people.
One of my best freinds of thirty years who was in the
top position of U.S. Navy when the Supreme Commander was U. S.
Army's Chief of Staff wrote me saying "you have a d-- good
man in your country today - Macarthur - He is big and he is
friendly. I can't think of a better man for the job he is
M
now doing." An American who came to Tokio recently, told me
that everyone did know Macarthur is a very fine soldier, but
now understands he is as well an
excellent statesman. whose
record will be written in the world's history. I knew the
General at Jashington, D. C., when he was Captain attached to
the ar Department and I was impressed with his personality
G
3
and thereafter foll wed his careers with interests and I share
these two views mentioned above completely.
6
1
Yow, ab ut my own country, I am sure of such opinion,
-2249
that Japan is destined to collapse financiully ind economically,
in comparatively near future, if we follow the present course,
as surely as the sun rise in the east ind set in the west.
Only way of saving from this calamity is by the concentration
of all efforts by Japanese in every walk of life for the single
urpose of savin the country, under the timely and most able
leadership of the Supreme Jonmander.
Jupan may be in better shape,
some yeurs hence, if helped by U.S.A.
I can't see no other way
left.
Needless to say, Japanese must do everything humanly
possible to help themselves but so ry to say, they are rather

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
The Future of Japan
Once we start war, there is a danger of bringing about
anaroly and bankruptoy in the post-war situation even if we
win the war.
The terrorism of Hitler is not something that last
forever.
We must
prepare the post-war plans. The World War II
occurred because of our mistake both in drafting the Versailles
Treaty and in managing the post-war situation.
This
time we must
not repeat the same mistake as we did before.
These were the opinions
of American statesmen.
They studied the post-war problems, step by
step, even in war-time.
This is what they call "Win the war, win
the peace!"
On the contrary, however, we Japanese determined to die
with our backs to
the wall if we couldn't win the war, and never
gave
a thought to post-war plans.
That is the economic situation of our contry today?
The production of commodities in Japan falls, far short of
the amount neceusury to maintain the minimum standard of living
for 80,000,000 peop1e.
As regards our food situation, we are no
longer in a position to import rice from Korea and Formosa, soya
beans (miso, soy sauce, and tofu
are
made from this bean) from
Manchuria and sugar from Formosa and
the ex-mandated South Sea
islands.
In Hokkaido and other home areas we have started to bring
waste land under cultivation. However, setting aside for the
moment the
scheduled cultivation of 1, 500,000 cho-bu (acres) of
land it is almost impossible to
expect a great contribution to
the urgent solution of our food problem if we, officials and
civilians alike, do not make geeat efforts to establish houses,
hospitals, schools, etc., grantin: that the use of machinery for
cultivation is not a problem as hitherto.
For the
time being
we must estimate the food shortage as being for one
or
two mon ths
a year in spite of the good hurvest this year.

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.98)
There is a shortage of 4 million houses because, in addition
to the aoute shortage of 2 million homes, 2 million more were
burnt.
To build these houses is a task whioh would be difficult
to accomplish in 10 or 20 years.
Japan lost everything and became extremely fatigued during
this long drawn-out war. Furthermore, we have to roam under the
heavy burden of reparations. We must acknowledge that the
reparations is a really heavy burden. It is known to all that
the coal production today is a great bottleneck in the reconst-
ruction of industries and the operation of railways. Te are now
trying to make up for this coal shortage partly by electric power,
but half of the thermal electric generators have been earmarked
for reparations.
Iron and steel as well as coal are the fundamental elements
of every industry, but their output is now very small in Japan.
Moreover, 70 or 80 of the production capacity will go towards
reparations. We cannot predict when we can again build ferro-
concrete houses or iron bridges by ourselves.
It is hard to repair the worn-out railways. More than 90
of the ship-building fucilities will go for reparations, leaving
construction facilities for only 150, 000 tons.
Before the var, shippin Was one of Jupan's rides, second
only to that of the Us: and Britain. e c uld see our slips with
the rising-sun flag all over the world, London, New York, Rio de
Janeiro, uenos lires, Sydney, Lelbourne, etc.
But this is now an
old tale.
en shall we again see the risin -sun lc in these
ports?
All the aluminium  rotrein facilities will o for reparations,
and the lo ses to our chemical industry are reat.
Our spianin industry has just be un to revive by importing
American raw cotton. Kowever, it is just like job-working because it
is controlled by the V. S. Army.
But even then we must be thankful
because it is helping us to recover.

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
3.
In short, post-war Japan with a population far greater than
its ability to support is in a position not unlike that of a head
of a household trying to support a large family, although lacking
the ability to do so.
This gives rise to the population problem.
Together with the necessity of cultivating national strength and of
supporting as great a population as possible, there arises the prob-
lem of ad justing the population so that it will not be increased
beyond the capacity of the nation to sulport.
We must realize that
Japan is today in a very weakened condition and sorely in need of
some
effective remedy which will bring her back to normal condi tions.
This effective remedy is nothing other than our wholehearted efforts
to increase production and to strive daily for the improvement of
our minimum livelihood.
Since remaining post-war stocks of various
commodities today have already touched bottom, we must at all costs
look to
the Uni ted States and other countries for aid in supplying
us wi th
those commodities which we are not able to provide for our-
selves.
It is of no use to raise wages if commodities are lacking.
Even though the 5o0-yen limit is raised to a thousand, this would
mean nothing, so long as it merely meant that the prices of commo-
dities doubled also, or it would mean that even a
thousand yen would
be insufficient if the prices tripled.
The prices
of commodities
today are continuing to rise.
At a time when even
the absolute
minimum requirements are lacking, the risin: wages in all fields
naturally means
an increase in the prices of commodities also. Thus,
wages and
the prices of commodities are playing a sort of see-saw
game and gradually developing into endless inflation.
The raising
of wages, therefore, does not by any means
serve as a fundamental
settlement of the question.
In a vost-war Japan which is unable to produce even one-fifth
of its pre-war production, we must first of all awaken spiritually

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
4.
and strive to inorease prodution, while realizing that there will
always be a shortage of commodities which must be endured. This is
the only road towards the revival of Japan.
Thoughtless strikes and
sabotage only mean that production will be further lowered and that
inflation will become increasingly severe, until finally it will
result in
the disgraceful enactment of a stru,gle in which society
finds itself at odds with one another for selfish gains.
Japan will
thus fall into a bottomless pit from which it will be impossible for
her to arise.
Heretofore, our foresight into the future has not always been
accurate.
In the European war, Japan was too eager in her attempts
lest she "miss the bus", so to speak.
She did not even have the
patience to verify clearly the destination of the bus.
There should
have been two views with regards to
the results of the Russo-German
war.
One of
these was that the war
would end in a complete victory
for Cermany within a few months, as Germany claimed.
The o ther was
that it would be a repetition of Mapoleon's failure or a repetition
of the failure of the war against Chungking.
However, our Government
subjectively took
the
former view.
This was
one of
the reasons for
its eagerness not to
"miss the bus".
gain, another example of
the same kind, because the Anglo-Saxon peoples hate war, we felt that
we could win
the war if we possessed a determined spirit, success-
fully managed
the "co-prosperity sphere" und attained self-sufficiency.
This was
one way of lookin at it.
In addition to
this hatred of war,
the democracies would be placed at a disadvan tage in the early stages
of the war because of
their psycholo ical unpreparedness for war.
But they do not acknowledge defeat because of one or two failures.
On the contrary, they would solidify their fighting spirit, conduet
a vast mobilisation of their natural resources and make a large-
scale counter-offensive.
This is a fact wiich is borne out by history.

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
5.
Therefore, it is dangerous to start a war thoughtlessly taking into
consideration only the victories in the early stages of the war.
Unless there exist well-founded plans for a long drawn-out war, it
is impossible to start a war so easily. If it is to be a long
dwawn-out war, it wauld become very disadvantageous to a country who
is poor in natural resources such as ours. Not only that but
politically it is clear that it would be affording an opportunity
to third-power nations such as the Soviet Union. This is also
another way of looking at the question.
An imerican admiral, a friend of mine, pointed out that the
Tri-Partite iact was unnatural.
He doubted that a military power
and a naval power which were
so far apart geographically could
effectively help each other.
It would be merely natural for Japan
to ally herself with nations possessing sea power such as the
United States and reat Britain. He went on to say that Japan, by
preservin intact one of the three most powerful navies, could by
that fact al ne possess an influential voice in any peace conference.
I myself was quite in agreement vith his views but because of my
position as ambassacor, I refrained from
expressing vooal agreement.
Cur country,in considering the several alternatives offered, made
its decision suhjectively based on wishful thinking rather than by
calmly grasping the true facts.
It became evident later that this
method was based on an illusion.
Similar to her views with regard
to war, her views with regard to peace are a lso very naive.
There
existed considerable evidence that Japan considered the -otsdam
Declaration
to suit her own advantage.
Then it gradually became
known to
the general public that this declaration was one which
possessed a severity hitherto unknown with regard to a defeated
nation. This is seen in the fact that official and private property
in foreign countries amounting to more than one hundred billion yen
have entirely been lost; that Japan's territory has been reduced to

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
6.
half; that the army, navy and airforce have been prohibited; that all
military and naval arsenals, all facilities for manufaoturing arms
and airplanes, 80% of steel producing facilities, more than 90% of
ball bearing, 50% of machine tools, in other words about 75% of
all her heavy industries, have become the object to reparations.
Facking and crating for shipping plants of maohinery for reparations
alone involve a great task costing forty billion yen.
We also lack the quality of thinking rationally of things.
It is also true in school education wherein the pupils are only made
to memorise. No efforts are made to go beyond this, to enable the
pupils to grasp the truth as a result of meditation. It is indeed,
a very out-moded system, especially when it comes to political
matters. It is regretful but true that those who study the new
Constitution and are interested in it number only a sad few among
the people.
As a result of this fact the people are always dragged along
by a few active leaders--regardless of whether these leaders be
good or bad. Viewed politically, the people are led like sheep
by the old feudal clans, or ut other times, by the political parties
or by the militury clique.
The present numerous labor movements
mi ht be classified as just one of these instances. and today they
are content to let matters go on just as the occupation army directs.
However, the occupution forces no doubt cannot depend on such
people.
We must abandon our old opportunist policy, possess a new
strong belief, and foster our ability to decide properly between
right and wrong. e are however, not so evil as a few foreigners
seem
believe nor are we people entirely lacking in common sense.
If we
ire dra ged uround in the wron direction so much, there comes
a time when we will gradually uwaken from the errors we have made.
The pendulum today is swining from the right to
the extreme left
but in a year or two it will no doubt swing back to the right.
during this time, nitional society will increasingly crumble, and

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
7.
economic difficulties will continue with serious conditions
aggravating.
Therefore, before we are pulled around too much, we
must regain our common sense.
Some of the high Covernment officials
are saying in a breezy manner thut the  rices of commodities are
stablised, that currency in circulation is not as great as the
figures vould show because lar e part seems to lie hoarded in
the homes of
the people and that
therefore there is nothing to
worry about.
If we really could
be so optimistic, it would be
most fortunite indeed.
Lowever,
they are setting up a very loose
financial plan which exceeds l00 billion yen.
They claim they can
cope with the revenue in this regard from the capital levy this year,
which is
be collected all at one time.
For the next fisoal year,
they say that by encouraging sivins from now they will make up the
deficit -- I don't know what this would amount to but considering
the
tremendous occupation costs as well as the
transportation costs
for
removin the facilities set aside for reparations, the figures
are bound to astronomical.
If the savings campaign does not
succeed,
there would he no
o ther way left but
to resort to excessive
issue of
bank notes as is practiced in Uhina.
The rate
of the
Chinese
currency to
the American dollar is some 5,000 to
1 at present.
Who can uarantee that Japan will not be pluced in a similar
condition?
Did not the Russian ruble and the German
mark become
almost valueless followiny the first World War?
In the United States, it is reported
that discussions are
taking place to alleviate taxes and to redeem public bonds, aiming
at a so und budget of about 30 billion dollars.
Japan's financial
and economic ability amounts to perhaps 1/20 of that of
the United
States.
There exist,
therefore, natural limits
to
the amount of
the budget which she can use during one year.
Can we remain
indifferent and allow the
compiling of a budget including occupation
costs and transportation costs for removing reparation facilities
which far
exceeds
the national ability?

In Soviet Russia also the fourth five-year plan has already
been announoed and the results of its first year have been
officially reported. When sovereignty was restored to the Emperor
by the Meiji Restoration and the feudal system gave away to the new
prefectural system, there was a short period of domestio confusion.
About this time, Koin Kido, one of the three big heroes of the
Restoration and grand father's of war guilt Kido, who is now facing
the trial at the International Military Tribunal, dedicated an ode
to a dead friend. This ode became a favorite of later generations.
I happened to see it written on a scroll when I travelled through
Sapporo the other day and I copied it.
The lone candle shines cold and bright before my eyes
As it sit and think with infinite feeling.
I turn my head and look for my old friend,
But he is no more.
Should not man necessarily strive for fame?
"ith the country in turmoil for long years
And tens of thousands dead,
The Imperial lalace has changed its aspects a number of times.
The years are flowing like water, never to return,
And man resembles grass or tree, competing for the fleeting
glory of spring.
The future fate of our conntry is by no means an easy one.
What shoul be done with the thirty millions of our people?
I sit in the mounta ins in the middle of the night,
Unable to sleep;
And the mountains are filled vith the sound of wind and rain.
At that time, our population vas some 30 millions, but now it
is about 80 millions. Furthermore, our territory has been reduced.
Even at the time of the Ieiji Restoration, Kido entertained grave
apprehensions with regurd to the future of the country, but now our
difficulties are ten times reater than at that time.
We must deeply reflect and repent on this tragic situation Which
is the result of our past crimes, and we must proceed on the road to
a new peace with valour.
Ever since the termination of War, we have been more or less
dumbfounded und have done nothing. This may be an inevitable situa-
tion. But let us not talk of the past, and as for the future, let

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.99)
9.
us utilise to the utmost our productive powers and our land both
of which have been reduced to half. By doubling and tripling our
efforts let us aim for self-sufficienoy and make up for the
inevitable deficit by trade. The situation is there is a lack of
materials and a surplus of manpower. Therefore, we must for the next
few years be resigned to the fact that the insufficiency will last
for some time. Thus we must try to promote our production as much
as possible and obtain the help of the occupation foroes in things
which we cannot by ourselves accomplish and put up with the minimum
standard of living. Ye must proceed step by step by our
own sweat
and strive to improve the situation.
At a time when the absolute quantity is insufficient, the
social equilibrium would be wrecked and the whole nation would be
thrown into confusion if one group shauld pursue their own selfish
ends.
It is a time in which morality in Japan has degraded and the
honest are always losing. Iropriety and courtesy have vanished and
freedom is practised as license. Burglary has increased and no
doubt this reveals the actual situation in all strata of society.
This situation can be a source of social revolution. Therefore, we
must develop our ability in order to be able to judge all kinds of
ideologies and their merits and demerits. I feel greatly encouraged
that there are a few partiots who do not lose their self-respect and
will to help themselves and who by this are trying to improve the
national and social situation.
There are aspects in which we must
proceed with caution but on the other hand we must not be
overly
pessimistic.
After t e first Morld Tar, Russia, Italy, ind Germany vere
hit to the depths by poverty and internal confusion and the
necessity for the
iron-handed policy of Stalin, Mussolini and
Hitler was felt.
These countries invariably adopted totalitarian
policies.
But after seeing that Stalin was the only one who succeeded

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.96)
10.
there are factions that claim there can be reconstruotion only after
destruction. As there would be many dying of starvation after the
destruction and this would necessitate many years even to initiate
reconstruction, we can not by any means agree with such an opinion.
Fost-war management is indeed difficult
but this diffioulty is
the responsibility imposed on our generation. According to the new
Constitution the sovereignty lies with the people and the Diet is
the Supreme organ of the national power. If that is the case, the
great work of reconstruction of the country lies in the responsibility
of the entire nation. The old and young, men and women, all in

FW

Natural Resources Section
8 September 1949
SUBJECT: Results of Second Election of Local Agricultural Land
Commissions, 18 August 1949
1. On 3 September 1949, Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
furnished NR/A the preliminary official summary of the results of
the election of city, town, and village agricultural land commissions.
Election was held 18 August 1949 (as provided by Cabinet Order 224,
20 June 1949) to choose 10 commissioners for each of 11,O43 local
commissions. Under Agricultural Land Adjustment Law these local
commissions are entrusted with primary responsibility for control
of purchase. sale, and rental of agricultural lands, as well as for
classlfication and consolidation of holdings.
2. Eligible voters were divided into three categories:
a. Category 1:  farmers who lease and cultivate more than
2 tan (Hokkaido, 5 tan) of agricultural
land.
b. Category 2: farmers who lease to cultivators more than
2 tan (Hokkaido, 5 tan) of agrieultural
land.
c. Category 3:
cultivators not inoluded under Category 1
or 20
These categories replace the old categories of tenants, landlords, and
owner cultivators, who, in the original elections of land commissions
held between 20 and 31 December 1946, were represented by 5. 3, and 2
commisstoners, respectively. The shift in ownership of agrioultural
land which resulted from the land reform program reduced the peroentage
of agricultural land cultivated by tenant farmers from 46 to 12 per-
cent. This change required that categories be re-defined and repre-
sentation on commissions be changed to conform to the new pattern of
ownership and cultivation.
3. The Local Autonomy Law (Law No. 67, 17 April 1947, as amended)
provides that:
a。

a. Where the number of eligible candidates for each category
equals the number of vacancies, no election shall be held, and the
candidates shall be declared automatically elected.
b. Where fewer candidates run for a given category than the
number of vacancies, a subsequent re-election must be held.
4. The Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry report summarized
as inclosures 1 and 2, is a preliminary report and contains a few
minor inconsistencies but is substantially correct.
. 5. Of a total of 11, O43 city, town, and village precincts, no
election took place (in accordance with par 3r. above) in 4,625 pre-
cincts, or 42 percent of the total (see inclc re 1). The number of
precincts in which no vot ing occurred varied widely by prefectures,
ranging from 10 percent in Akita to 73 percent in Shiga. Details are
not now available, but Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry estimates
on the basis of data received that in approximately 30 percent of the
precincts, elections were held for all three categories. The high
percentage of automatic elections of candidates should not be assumed
to indicate necessarily either lack of interest or failure to achieve
a reasonably democratic choice of representatives. It is reported that
burakus generally appeared more concerned to secure representation on
a geographic basis than on the basis of the three categories,.and ac-
cordingly, in many instances decided in informal pre-election agreements
to throw their support solidly to selected candidates. The balanse of
the candidates usually withdrew promptly to avoid probable defeat and
conseguent loss of face. In general, competition for office. was most
active in outlying districts.

that where electians were held, approximately 78 peroent of all those
eligible voted. The percent of eligible men who voted exceeded women
in the same catogory by a substantial amount, though of the votes cast,
approximately 45 percent were cast by women. As in the first election,
about l00 women geined seats. almost without exception representing.
category 2, the owner class.

SUBJECT:
Tokyo's 620, September 9, 1949
POLAD forwards a significant article in a Japanese
periodical by Fuse Katsuji on "Chances for a Bolshevik Revo-
lution in Japan". Fuse is a noted authority on Soviet affafrg
OC/R
This Document
and has had interviews with both Lenin and Stalin. After
reviewing Soviet policy objectives on the Asiatic mainland,
Japanese conditions and those which prevailed in Russia in l9i7.
Both countries, he points out, had emerged from disastrous mili-
tary adventures resulting in acute economic dislocation.
In both instances, he says, the discredited militaristic
bureaucracies were supplanted by liberal regimes (Kerensky
Government and the Japanese occupation. As the Kerensky
Government succumbed to the Bolshevik revolution in Novembet, 19l
in the face of labor and agrarian unrest, so do the Communists
hope that through an all-out labor offensive the Communists ll
come to power in Japan. Fuse also sees & parallel between
Lenin's use of German PoW's in Russia to undermine the Kerene
Government and present Soviet use of Japanese repatriates in
8
Japan.
49
According to Fuse, the Soviets will concentrate upon the
oppressed nation concept--oppressed by U.S. "imperialist
colonization"--in setting the stage for their all-out effort
at bolshevizing Japan.

Subject: Foreign Office Populer Lecture Program.
ACTION
FE
The Acting Folitical Adviser has the honor to refer
to his despatch no. 590 cf August 27, 1949, transmitting
INFOR
a memorandum regarding the Foreign Ministry's lecture
DCR
progrem in Hokkaido, and to enclose a second memorandum
OLI
prepared by a member of the Japanese Ministry of Foreign
POS
Affairs summarizing observations made during the course
EUR/x
of further lectures on the international situation. The
EUR
purpose of these lectures is to combat Soviet propaganda,
OFD
which is directed chiefly against the United States and
ITP
spread by "Akahata" and other Communist publications, by
Party meetings, and in various other ways.
c
During the period from July 20 to August 31, the
Foreign Office sent out 56 lecturers, who gave 3i2 lec-
tures. These meetings have apparentiy been well received
and have excited lively interest.
As is natural, many of the questions put to the
speakers coincided with those put in Hokkaido In addi-
tion to such questions as whether the United States would
withdraw from Japan in the event of war with the Soviet
Union, questioners manifested considerable preoccupation
with Occupation and economic problems. Identical ques-
tions were raised in different places as to the compara-
tive figures for the costs of American aid to Japen and
of the costs of the Occupation to Japan, and severel
queries revealed a fear that Japan might become a mercan-
tile colony of the United States. Questions on the
United States dealt largely with labor problems, while
those on the U.S. S.R. showed the effect of the plausible
half-truths of Communist propaganda.

(COPY)
LECTURE-MEETING FOR PUBLIC ENLIGHTENMENT
ON INTERNATIONAL SITUATION
In response to recuests from various local communities
the Ministry of Foreign Affairs has sent out lecturers
from tine to time since August l946, in an effort contribute
to a more corred public understanding of the drift of the
world situation (See Annex A).. In view of the fact that
a group of extremists have been spreading, of late, false
and biased information on the international situation for
propaganda purposes (through "The Akahata" and similar
publications) with the intention of creating confusion in
the public mind and aggravating the social situation in
this country, the Ministry has made a plan, which it is
now carrying out, to send out lecturers to lecture-meet-
ings throughout the country to enlighten the public on
the international situation. with the aim of enabling the
general public more effectively to have a clearer objective
understanding of how the world is moving.
1。
The first lecture-meetings are to be held between
the end of July and October.
2。
As a rule, meetings will be held at 15 to 16 commu-
nities in each prefecture. In addition to meetings
in large cities, much stress will be laid on lec-
tures in smaller cities and towns as well as farm-
villages.
3。
Meetings will be held under the auspices of cities,
towns, or villages or cultural and other organi-
zations and to these meetings the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs will send speakers.
As regards the lectures given between 20 July amd the
end of August. 56 lecturers were sent out and 312 lectures
were given in all as will be seen from Annex B. The
schedule of lectures for September is shown in Annex C.
The lecture-meetings have been favourably received and the
local public have been deeply impressed with the lectures.
Accordingly. the Ministry is planning to hold meetings of
a more effective nature, in resnonse to local requests,
as many times as the budgetary condition permits in order
to keep the public informed of current international events
and trends.
In Annex D

Annex D.
Important questions asked at lecture-meetings
---31 Aug., 1949, the Press Section, the Public Infor-
mation Division---
(Of the questions put to the lecturers sent by the
Ministry of Foreign Affairsto the lecture-meetings held
between 20 July and 31 August, those asked from a particular
thought point of view are shown hereunder.)
I. Political questions concerning Japan:
a。
Is the change of capitalistic countries to commu-
nistic countries not a natural consequence of
social evolution? (Nagasaki Prefecture)
b。
For what reasons has the Ministry of Foreign Affairs
planned lectures like the present one? (Fukushima
Prefecture)
c.
The lecturer is siding with America, but is it not
possible for us to try to understand our neighbour-
ing country Soviet Russia with a good will?
(Fukushima Prefecture)
d. Is it possible for the Communist Party of Japan to
come into power if they can hold an overwhelming
ma jority as a result of a future election? (Kochi
Prefecture)
e。
The Communist Party says that they would absolutely
not use violence in carrying out a revolution, but
are they really thinking so? (Sapporo)
f。
How does the American a id to Japan bear comparison
with the occupation expenditure we are paying?
(Ishikawa Pref.)
Note: The same question was asked at three other
places.
g。
Will America and Soviet Russia fight with each other
eventually? (Kochi Pref., Hokkaido, and Fukushima
Pref.)
h。
Will the U.S. Army evacuate from Japan in case of war
between America and Soviet Russia? (Bibai-machi,
Hokkaido)
1。
Soviet Russie is on the defensive in respect to
America and it is America, I feel, who is a warlike
nation. but Soviet Russia appears to be pacific.
What is your view on that point? (Nagasaki)
2. Questions concerning Asia:
It is felt that the occupation of South Korea by
North Korea may have an important bearing upon Japan.
How do you think about it? (Ishikawa Pref.)--the
same question was asked at two other
places.

Tokyo, subject: "Forelgn Offine
Primacy.over Missions Perforaed
Abroadi
COPY
DECISION OF CABINET MEÉTING
October 4th, 1949.
Re Dispatch of Personnel Abroad for Participation in
International Conferences and for Discharge of Other
Official Duties.
In case Government officials or corresponding personnel thereof
are to be dispatched abroad for the purpose of participating in
international conferences and of discharging other official duties,
the Ministers concermed shall reach a mutual agreement prior to its
operation with the Minister for Foreign. Affairs.
(Reasons)
It is believed that the occasion will gradually increase in
the future to dispatch Government officials or corresponding per-
sonnel thereof to attend international conferences and to discharge
other official duties and, therefore, it is not only essential to
obtain a maximum efficiency and to avoid duplication in appropriating
the necessary funds, but it is also urgently needed, in view of the
delicate international situation, to carry out the handling of the
matter with utmost discretion and in complete unity of action among
Japanese Government Agencies concerned.

DCR

Subject:
Transmitting Copy of "Brief Progress Report
on Political Reorientation of Japan", Pre-
scm
pared by Government Section- of General Head-
quarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers.
The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to
transmit herewith a single copy of a document entitled
"Brief Progress Report on the Political Reorientetion
of Japan" prepared by the Government Section, General
Headquarters Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
and released October 12. 1949 It is understood that
additional copies will be made available to the Depart-
ment through the usual distribution channels of the
Department of the Army.
The first six chapters of the report, covering pages
1-3l contain considerable f ctual data on the pre-war
government of Japan and the major changes that have taken
place since the surrender in the direction of represen-
tative democracy. The last two chapters (pages 32-47)
describe and illustrate the underlying philosophy that
has guided General MacArthur as well as his modus oper-
andi under that philosophy. The report also reproduces
(pages 48 62) the Constitution of Japan.
Evaluations of the political reorientation of the
Japanese occur from time to time in the report, of which
the two most comprehensive are as follows:
".. the political reorientation of Japanhas
progressed far beyond the initial and necessary
stages of changes in leadership, revision of
governmental processes and institutions and has
2
now reached
RESTRICTED

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.97)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 731,
Octoher 20, 1949.
RESTRICTED
-2-
now reached the stage where stability derives from
an increasingly informed electorate conscious of its
political power and responsibility." (Page 31)
"Few of the measures taken in the reformation of
the Japanese governmental structure or body of
law. to conform to the requirements of a free
and democratic society, bear the stamp or 'stigma:
of Allied force. Those high concepts thus injected
into Japanese life institutiona and thought should
endure long after the occupation has been with-
drawn and MacArthur's philosophy, without prece-
dent in the annals of military occupations of the
past. will live as a standurd and a challenge to mili-
tary occupat ions of the future." (Page 36.)
Without disparaging in any way the achievements actu-
ally made in the politicel reorientation of Japan, it is
apparent thst the reforms outlined are as yet too new to
have proved their permanence. It also appears that the
Government Section has somewhat minimized its role in
the revision of governmental processes and institutions.
Moreover the great role played by General MacArthur's
personal leadership, as well as the inclination of the
Japanese to follow the lead of the Occupation while it
continues, must be regarded as contributing to the pre-
sent cooperativeness of the Japanese, but which will not
necessarily assure the permanence of the reforms now being
carried out under the Occupation.
