Enclosure to Despatch no. 738
dated October 22, 1949 from
the United States Political Adviser
for Japan, Tokyo, on the subject
"Transmitting Memorandum on "Local
Autonomy Program for the cities of
Ja an".
CONFIDENTIAL (For Department's use only)
MEMORANDUM REPORT
October 10, 1949
SUBJECT:
Local Autonomy Program for the Cities of Japan
as Conducted by Legal and Government Division,
Civil Affairs Section, Headquarters Eighth Army
-- Conference at City of Uji-Yamada
The Legal and Government Division of the Civil Affairs
Section, Headquarters Eighth Army, has been engaged in
presenting a series of conferences to educate Japanese
officials and the general public in their rights and duties
under democracy. These conferences were first held on the
Prefectural level in the early part of this year. The pre-
sent series, devoted to the local autonomy program for the
cities of Japan, began in mid-September and will continue
until the end of December. They are being held at princi-
pal cities not covered previously on the Prefectural level.
The program is implemented by two teams whose leaders
are high-ranking officials in the Legal and Government
Division. In some cases the Chief of the Division, Mr.
Cecil B. Tilton, has been in charge. Additional persons
are drawn either from the Division or from personnel of the
local Military Government Tean. In order to insure the
proper presentation of certain basic principles of local
autonomous government, the Legal and Government Division
has prepared five speeches which are delivered on the first
day of the conference.
The first speech (Enclosure 1), entitled "The Citizen
in Japan", is devoted to a discussion of the rights and
duties of the citizen toward his city as a voter and as a
tax payer. The subject is developed in light of the Anglo-
American tradition, the new Japanese constitution and the
new local autonomy law (Chiho Jichi Ho). The second speech
(Enclosure 2) is devoted to the office of mayor with parti-
cular reference to the historical background and to the
various powers and functions of the mayor in Japan under
the local autonomy law. Particular emphasis is laid on
the relationship between the mayor and the city assembly,
and the mayor and the governor, both of which have been a
major source of trouble in the new Japan. The third speech
(Enclosure 3) treats of the City Assembly in Japan under
the new local autonomy law, with emphasis on the powers of
the Assembly, the people's duty toward the Assembly and
the Assembly's relation with the mayor, city committees
boards and commissions. The fourth speech (Enclosure 4)
is a discussion of municipal administration in terms of
policy, relationship with the people, specific services,
general
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
general principles of organization and specific instances
of city practices in Japan. The final speech (Enclosure
5) is devoted to committees, commissions, boards as inde-
pendent agencies in local government. Because these
organizations are new to Japan, considerable space is
given to their definition, their character, their legal
functions, their place in local government, and their
relationship with the courts in terms of specific boards
and commissions.
Subsequent to the preparation of these speeches in
the Division, they were translated into Japanese by a
Japanese judge and reviewed by a former Japanese diplo-
mat. It is the policy of the Division that these speeches
shall be presented as nearly verbatim as possible with
interpreters reading only the approved translations.
Conference at Uji-Yamada
The conference at Uji-Yamada which I attended is
typical of the series now being conducted by the Division.
It will be of interest to note the methods used, the prob-
lems broached in discus sion and audience reaction. By
this means some judgement can be made regarding the effect-
iveness of the conferences and the .progress of democratiza-
tion in Japan.
The Team at the present conference was headed by Mr.
Howard D, Porter, a Cincinnati lawyer in private life and
now one of the high-ranking Department of the Army civil-
ian employees in the Legal and Government Division. His
second in command was Major Charles R. Thomas, Jr., a
younger, regular Army officer whom I found to be both com-
petent and very sympathetically int erested in the work.
Sergeant First Class William E. Hurtig, who has spent
approximately three years with the Miye Prefecture Mili-
tary Government Team, also took part in the conference.
Because of an unforeseen shorta e in team personnel, the
writer, originally an observer, was asked to participate
in the work and was asked to read the first speech in
Japanese and to lead the discussion roups relative to
Speech No. 1.
The quality of inter retin was in general very good.
An accomplished interpreter, educated at the University of
Southern California and Keio University, Tokyo, was pro-
vided for Major Thomas. The Miye Military Government Team
supplied two additional interpreters, one a Japanese
graduate of Harvard, the other a former banker.
Initial preparations had been made by letter inform-
ing the mayor of Uji-Yamada of the Conference, its contents
and necessary groundwork. The group arrived at Uji-Yamada
on Saturday, October 1, and was met at the train by the
mayor, assistant mayor and police chief. After exchange of
greetings we proceeded to the City Hall. We then spent
about an hour and a half coing over the preparations the
city officials had made. One of the problems, Mr. Porter
informed me, is the tendency of city officials to adhere
to the old
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 3 -
to the old practice of featuring short speeches from
Prefectural officials at these meetings. Porter attempted
to dissuade the mayor from having too many of these
speeches but in the end the governor and the head of the
Prefectural Assembly did make speeches at the opening
ceremony on Monday morning. The mayor explained that
he had already sent out invitations for a short speech
from them and could not withdraw these invitations.
During the course of the day the writer had an opport-
unity to discuss the city and its affairs with the mayor
and vice-mayor. The Mayor, Mr. Takao Keiya, was elected
less than one month ago. Although born in Uji-Yamada, he
has spent about twenty years as a Japanese government offic-
ial in Formosa, and served in all parts of the Island. Prior
to becoming mayor he was a member of the City Works Com-
mittee. Judging fron the way they handled the conference,
both the mayor and his assistant seemed to be fairly com-
petent city officials. The Vice-Mayor, Mr. Misao Nagasumi,
first lived in Kyoto and moved to Uji-Yamada more than
thirty years ago. He has spent approximately thirty years
in various positions at the City Office. According to
these gentlemen, Uji-Yamada's principal business has con-
sisted in catering to visitors to the Grand Shrine of Ise
(first shrine of Japan) which adjoins the city. There is
no industry to speak of; most of the limited land is de-
voted to hotels and smail shops. During the latter part
of the war the city was bombed and although by area only
forty-seven percent was demolished, the city officials
claim that this area accounted for about eighty percent of
the town's income. At the present time they said they
have only forty percent of their pre-war tourist trade.
This is causing a very acute eccnomic problem among the
city inhabitants.
The city officials also told me the Grand Shrine of
Ise is suffering financial difficulties. In normal times
the shrine is rebuilt every twenty years. This year, be-
cause of lack of funds, the new shrine is not being con-
structed. Only the bridge crossing the river to the shrine
is being rebuilt. It might be noted parenthetically that
as an aftermath of the war and laws separating church and.
state, Ise, along with other Shinto shrines, has experienced
a severe drop in the number of visitors.
Turning to politics the city officials stated that
there were only two thousand Communist votes at the last
election and tihat the party is on the decrease. This was
particularly noticable after the Communists refused to allow
one repatriot from Siberia to see his family prior to going
to Communist Farty headquarters. The strongest party is
the Conservative Democratic Liberal Party.
During the weekend I had the opportunity to talk in-
formally with n. Forter and Major Thomas about the evalua-
tion of the speeches and their effectiveness. Both took me
completely
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 4 -
completely into their confidence and gave me all possible
assistance during this trip. Porter and Thomas felt some
of the speeches were over the heads of the people. They
believed that material of about the standard of sth grade
civics lessions was needed; that the papers tended to give
too much historical background. They also said utilisa-
tion of the prepared speeches tended to put people to sleep
while the use of a living speech more directly related to
the daily problems of the people kept them interested.
They cautioned against being drawn into rendering an opin-
ion on some phase of local political contention in the
discussion groups.
The Conference: First Day
The conference convened on Monday, October 3, at
10:30 in the morning. Approximately 1,300 people assembled
in the Girls' High School auditorium for the opening cere-
monies and first day speeches. The audience included mayors
of the principal cities in Miye Prefecture, city assembly-
men, civic leaders and the interested general public. Many
were present by invitation of the mayor of Uji-Yamada. To
the left of the speakers' rostrum and facing the audience
were gathered city and prefectural officials. To the right
were seated members of the Team, the commanding officer of
Miye Military Government Team, Lt. Colonel Meek, and the
interpreters. Behind the speakers' rostrum were prominent-
ly posted two large charts with captions in both Japanese
and English, one entitled "Outline of City Government" and
the other, "The Arch of Good City Government." To the left
and right, attached to the top of the proscenium, was the
progran for the day. The Mayor of Uji-Yamada opened the
meeting with a short speech about the necessity for know-
ledge of regional autonomy under the new law. This was
followed by short speeches from Governor Osamu Aoki, Kyukichi
Kokubo, Chief of the City Assembly, the Chief of the Pre-
fectural Assembly, and a representative of the other Miye
Prefecture city mayors and assembly heads who were merely
introduced. Next, Lt. Colonel Meek was introduced and made
a brief speech about his confidence in Miye Prefecture and
in the progress which it has made under his predecessor and
himself. He then briefly introduced the members of the Team.
Mr. Porter followed with a speech in which he told of
General MacArthur's reat interest in this type of confer-
ence. He stressed the fact that it was only two years since
local autonomy, that good progress had been made and that
the people should not be discouraged at any difficulties or
errors which night have been encountered, but rather should
keep striving to correct them and strugle to achieve true
local autonomy. He pointed out that the first day would be
devoted to speeches, but the most inportant part was the
second day during which the people could and should ask all
possible questions. Upon conclusion of Mr. Porter's re-
marks, the writer was introduced and read Speech No. 1 in
Japanese. This consists of twenty-one pages and the read-
ing time was approximately one hour and ten minutes. With
the conclusion
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 5 -
the conclusion of this speech, at about 12:40, the meet-
ing adjourned for luncheon.
The afternoon was devoted to a speech by Major Thomas
which, because of shortage of personnel and time, combined.
Speeches Nos. 2 and 4. He was followed by Sergeant Hurtig
who gave Speech No. 3 on the Assembly in Japan, omitting
a good part of the history and interposing two points of
particular local relevance. Considerable trouble has been
encountered through criticism by the public of assemblymen
in Miye Prefecture and attempts to oust them summarily from
office. Sergeant Hurtig pointed out that assemblymen were
not to be relieved from office until a trial by court had
been held. He also indicated that too many assemblymen
were resigning their positions for trivial or meaningless
reasons. He maue an addit ional point of the duties of city
officials in government assemblies with reference to dis-
orderly demonstrations. This portion of the speech was
primarily directed at the anti-tax demonstrations which
were frequent occurences in Japan last year.
The final speech of the day was made by Mr. Porter on
committees, commissions and boards. Porter followed the
content of Speech No. 5 but revised it considerably and
spoke as if extemporaneously without reference to the pre-
pared speech. He analyzed the various types of committees
and independent agencies (boards of education, public safe-
ty committee, etc.) found in the United States and indi-
cated whether or not they might better the local government
situation in Japan. He concluded his speech with a brief
reference to the Shoup report (proposing a new taxation
system for Japan) and point ed out the report sought to de-
vote a much larger percenta e of the tax income to local
government than to the prefectural covernments. Mr. Porter
privately explained to me that one of the most acute prob-
lems faced by local government has been the lack of income.
The local autonomy law has imposed new and heavier burdens
upon local goverament but the tax structure has not been
revised to provide the necessary funds. The police and
fire departments in particular have been having a very
difficult time for the past two years. In some instances
gasoline for fire trucks was lacking and there have been
other equally disturbin circumstances. Porter told me
that wherever possible he tried to commend these struggl-
ing agencies and point out to the public the difficult
problems they were facing, and thereby alleviate the tend-
ency of the people to be unduly critical of their officials.
Conference: Second Day
On the second day of the conference the audience, well
over 1,300 persons, was divided into four separate roups.
The four speakers moved from room to room and met with each
group for one hour and twenty minutes. The discussions
began at 9:20 in the morning and were oncluded at 3:50 in
the afternoon
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 6 -
the afternoon. One of the most interesting facts to
come out of the four discussion groups was the evident
tendency, particularly in the rural areas, to label as
"red" those who opposed the majority view in a free and
lawful discussion group. This would seem to be fairly
deep-rooted even at the present time. In order to coun-
ter this trend it was emphasized that all progressive
steps in democracy were based on free and lawful inter-
change of opinions and ultimately resulted in the bost
possible view bein. utilized for the good of the city.
Despite the fact that this area had considerable
indoctrination in democratic politics because one of the
grand old men of Japanese politics, Osaki, lived there,
it was still apparent a sense of civic duty and responsi-
bility was not well developed. The questions disclosed a
marked propensity on the part of the voters to lay civic
difficulties completely at the door of the officials and
a tendency to overlook the fact that they, themselves, were
responsible for electing such officials. It was also appar-
ent that there was a definite inclination on the part of
the people to be unduly critical of the city officials,
and a lack of appreciation that, regardless of the men in
office, a large number of their present-day problems, such
as taxation, are due to acute economic dislocations result-
ing from the war and, at the present time, that it is
impossible for anyone to offer a complete solution.
In connection with city administration, the most
poignant question pertained to taxation. To say the least,
the tax system in the city areas is extremely haphazard.
In every discussion roup one or nore persons revealed
that assessments varied extremely between individuals liv-
ing in the same area and enjoyin the same status. Appeals
to tax officials brought not even the courtesy of a hear-
ing. The problem is particularly ucute because, in many
instances, the people literally do not have funds avail-
able with which to pay the taxes.
Another problem of city dainistration is the lack
of funds. Under the present tax system relatively little
money is channeled to the city, the majority going to the
prefecture and the central overnment. The local autonomy
law imposes great net ourdens and responsibilities on the
city administration ith the result that many of the new
responsibilities go berins for the financial means to
carry them out. At least one question was directed to me
on this subject by a member of a fire department. Members
of the Team answered questions relative to tax problems by
citing the Shoup report and explained the new tax system
for Japan would allocate a much more substantial portion
of the tax income to the city anu, thereby, considerably
alleviate these difficulties.
Ot .er que tions showed the people had an incomplete
realization of the functions and duties of the mayor as
opposed to the city council. In some instances the mayor
and city
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 7 -
and city council are at complete loggerheads and the
mayor has been placed in an almost impossible position
through inability to claim the right to be present at
city meetings and to explain the course of action he
desires put into effect. The Mayor of Uji-Yamada said
he had difficulty with his Assembly and it refused to
authorize an official car, forcing him to ride a bicycle.
One problem of considerable interest reflecting the
separation of church and state concerned the right of
the city to preempt land belonging to the Ise Shrine for
a children's playground. The questioners claimed that
no other suitable land was available and the shrine
authorities had been approached but were unwilling to de-
vote any portion of their extensive land for this purpose.
A suggested solution was a compromise with the shrine
officials.
About eight percent of the audience consisted of
women. Not many asked questions. However, several asked
about the new position of women in Japan and the problems
they are encountering. The women seemed to feel the major
portion of their problem was sociological. Since the
social or family pattern in Japan has, up to very recently,
given the woman a very subordinate position in the home
and in society, the men do not recognize the woman's new
freedom under the law. The women feel it is most essential
for the men to change their way of thinking. They were
particularly interested in omen's political organizations
in the United States, the type of organization and scope of
activities. At the request of the local women's club, the
writer held a special meeting after the Conference ad-
journed for the purpose of a fuller expression and dis-
cussion of the se topics. The women apparently welcomed
one who could speak directly to them in Japanese. In brief,
the iapression is that women' organizations in Japan are
taking their first faltering step and will need some very
active pushing from ardent Japanese feminists if they are to
achieve for Japanese women what American women's civic
organizations have accouplished.
Ir. Porter reported he founa the people rather unre-
sponsive with questions. However, major Thomas encountered
a large number of stimulatin questions as did Sergeant
Hurtig and the riter. It is not surprisin that Porter
found this conditio. because committees and commissions
are not widely used at tne present time.
One of the particular problems the Team wi shed to
support was a ainst recall of city officials. Major
Thomas told me in private that last year r. Cecil Tilton,
Chief of the Legal and Government Division, had spoken
widely urgin utilization of the recall where the people
encountered
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 8 -
encountered bad officials. As a result of this campaign
some two hundred mayors were recalled before it was
realized the people were misusing this political device.
Now the emphasis is on moderation with the recall as a
last resort as in the United States. This is, in part,
a commentary on the still present responsiveness of the
people to direction.
The Conference closed with greetings from the Mayor,
from Lt. Colonel Meek and from Major Thomas of the Team
who read a letter indicating interest and support of the
problem from Major General Walton H. Walker, Commanding
General of the ighth Army.
Conclusions
From the above it may readily be seen that there is
an interest and desire among the people to learn about
and institute democratic practices and principles. How-
ever, in many phases this desire is impeded by old poli-
tical and sociological patterns. Thus, it may be anti-
cipated that the ultimate success of democracy in Japan
will be contingent on three factors (1) the present day
groundwork being laid, (2) education of school children
in democracy, and (3) solution of basic economic problems.
For the present it may be said that democracy is growing
but will require careful nurturing for a considerable
period. Its success will depend on the extent to which
the Japanese, themselves, have realized its benefits and
endeavor to put it into practice. At present there is a
somewhat noncritical acceptance of democratic teachings
but some segments seem critical about whether certain
aspects are applicaule to Japan. The crucial point will
come when the occupation leaves and reaction against the
period of military rule may ve anticipated. As far as
Miye Prefecture is concerned, I feel it will ultimately
emerge with a Japanese brand of democracy suited to
Japanese needs. In this context I feel the Occupation
will be successful in its democratization activities.

William L. Ma istretti
American Vice Consul

The Office of Mayer
The Office of Mayor in Jepan is the chief executive division of
city government. In the Office rests the authority for directing and
supervising the cperation of civic affairs. The man or woman, there-
fore, who becomes the meyor, is the controller of the execution of city
policy for the people. The men or woman who is elected mayor does not

In the early deys in the United States the nayor was merely the
presiding officer of the council, possessing little independent author-

meyor who was corrupt and interested only in furthering his political
fortune as against the voters' and taxpeyers' interests. As a result,
different groups of reforners developed to meet the situation. The
first group advoceted strengthening the office of mayor by a statute -
to give the mayor complete control over administration and to have the

electorate hold hin personelly responsible at the poll

Even with the election of a good mayor many instances of failure o
curred because the mayor was powerless to overcome hostile subordinates

The second group of reformers developed in Gelveston, Texas, in
1900, advocating a commission type of government. Under this system
the mayor was reduced to a position of minor importance. He became
merely the chairman of a board of five members, possessed only the seme
voting power as his fellow commissioners. he was charged, as was each
commissioner, with the administration of but one of the city's depart-
ments. This commission system proved populer end by 194l. 400 cities
and towns followed the plan. Shortly after, another group, the com
mission group, launched its idee, developed the council-manager plan of
city government in Dayton, Ohio vhich took administretive control com-
pletely out of the hands of the mayor and made him little more then a
presiding officer with certein social duties. The city manager is a
businessman and the fow elected councillors who eppoint him set the
policy for him to carry out with his menegerirl cbility. He is a mod-
ern version of the English tom clerk without the relative security of
tenure which the latter enjoys by virtue of the personnel system under
which he is employed.
The commission plan and the council-meneger plen have great advan-
tages and as types of government they exist in meny municipelities. The
lerge majority of Americen cities, however. have kept the strong mayor
type of city governnent. As egainst the denger thet the neyor will be-

office of mayor. When executive end administrative responsibility are
acettered in too many offices, boards end commissions, responsibility
is difficult to coordinate and is really non-existent on many occasions.
Under the strong mayor plan, the executive authority is soncentrated,
and not diffused. A responsible executive is placed in charge of admin-
istrative matters. Government is made intelligible to the average citi-
zen. pportunities for shifting the blame in bobbled matters are vir-
tually eliminated. The council is given just one task, that of policy
determination or legislation end is not permitted to interfere with the
technical details of administration. Concentration, simplicity and con-
fidence, principles of good government, are all embodied in the strong
mayor plan.

The Assembly in Japan
Thc successful operation of city assemblies in the nation is of
vital importance to the long range objective for Japan to implant and
keep sound and healthy representetive covernont. The new assemblies
have real power and authority as contrasted with the impotent and weak
character of pre-war assemblies. To the extent that the assemblies
actuelly use end exorcise their presently established functions, can
the success of representative govemnent be measured in cities. To
the extent that they develep denocratic leadership in local govemnent,
cen the cities command respect and secure desirable reputations. The
Chihojichiho or Law Concerning Local Autoncmy creates assemblies as de-
liberative argans conposed of rerresentatives clected by direct popular
vote of the pecple. The old lsws provided for direct elective represen-
tetion but they certainly did not prcvice for the assemblyman to do any-
thing after he was eleeted. The assembl ies are vested with a range of
local functions to permit and enecurege a maximun of individual thought.
initiative and judgment in the management of their local comunity affairs.
One significant fact vill be renembered. The assombly as an insti-
tution in the United States and Inglish countries, is the foundation of
representative covermment. In England and in the United States the
greatest interest has been generated by the position in governnent of
the assembly as a deliberative body. In England the very existence of
the national and lecal governments rosts upon the power and funetions
of the free assembly - the perliament and the Iccel council. In the

So, too, in Japan today tho contral government through a national law

e public carporation because the state establishod it as an aiding in-

term corporation here meens the body or legal azenay which is to act for

civil corporation and political cornoration, mean the same thing as pub-

the municipal corporation is a body politic. This term means that all
the people of the district are braught togother legally by the state to
be one person, to bo one body, and that this person, this body, will act
to benefit and to assist all of those poople. In doing this very thing.
it will benefit and assist the stato's overnent as a whole. Moreover,
the body politic has life. It, therofore. lives. It has continuity;
its legal nature permits it to go right along goverming and acting for
the people yuar after year. The municipality in Japan is a juridical
porson under the Chihojichihn; hence, th city has a life of its own.
It can ovm property and exercise its legal rights and responsibilities
like a city in Aericu or in England, Australia, New Zealand or Canada.

Powers of the City Ansembly
The povers and authcrity of the city assembly are essentially
legislative, they are also investigative. In the conduct of the city's
affairs the assembly is often in need of facts to enact effectivo by-laws

mentary to the legislative.

or resolutions and jorei or by-laws. The giketsu is an intention of
the assembly; it is an interral affair of the assembly and under normal
conditions the duties or rights of the citizcn are not affected. The
oxeeption occurs only when the riketsu has been promulgated in a fixed
form; in this special cheracter it can heve similer cffects to the by-
law. The jorei is the by-law. The purpose in enacting it is to produco
same type of public action. It, therefore, does affect the rights or
cuties of the citizon.

The by-laws which are enacted by the city assembly must legally
come within the Constitution, the laws passed by the Diet and the by-
laws enacted by the prefectural covermnents. This legal position in
Japan of the city's by-laus is similer to that in the United States and
in Englend. The Constitution of the United States establishes certain
powers and affairs for the federal or national government end lodges
all remaining povers in the state governments. The State Constitutions
and laws in their turn, make divisions and refinements. They do so in

the creation of the cities by enumeration and saparation

In England, the Administrative County, of which there are 62 in-
cluding London, was created by the Act of 1888 as the largest terri-
torial area of local governnent. The extent of the County Counoil's
powers in some functions is limited within its territory because of
(1) other local entities and (2) established legal relations among all
the autonomous bodies. Thus, the County Council's power in Elementary
Education "stops short" of the Municipal Boroughs whose population is
10,000 or over and of the Urban Districts vhose population is 20,000
or over. The County Council's police powers "stop short" of Municipal
Boroughs whose population is over 10,O00. The County Council's powers
on important road matters "stop short" of Boroughs, of Urban Districts
and Rural Districts. Stated in another way as betveen the various local
governnents within the county on the one hand and the county government
itself on the other hand, there is a division and separation of some
administrative and financial affairs.
This situation is sinrilar to that of the police system in Japan
in which the power of tho National Rural Folice "stops short" of all
toms and villcges of 5.o0o or more. Many similar caparisons will be
found in the United Statos.

Municipal Administration
Adninistretion is the heect of the execative brench of government.
The universel use cf che tern anministreli  in municipal government meens
not only the officers and other employees of the city's organization but
also the structure of offices in which all these people perforn their duties.
People can frequently be heard to say, "I think thet the edministration hes
done well this year," or "Kobayashi-Kun belongs to the edministration." In
this case people ere tolking ebout the mayor's policy end the chief assis-
tants. In enother situation, the people ney say, "This city's edministration
is very simply formed," or "What does the edninistretion look like?" In this
situetion they ere talking about the departments, bureaus, sections or units
1
and offices into which the city government is divided.
hile the orgenization of sections end units end office structure of
7
municipal government is inportent, no guarentee in them alone exists that
perfection can be achieved. However good the crganization and structure may
be, the ultinate success of good adninistretion depends upon the officials
end enployees. In city government, as in ell forms of hunan activity, good
results are the factors that count. The nuaber of edministrative officials,
assenbly mer bers, stending conmittees or indenendent conmissions or boerds,
while inportant, are not the imnediate euides to rating or ranking the suc-
cess of the city administration. Factors such as schools, low crime retes,
the decrease of illiteracy, the number of well paved, cleened end lighted
streets, well cleaned street drairs, sidewalks, sanitational systems, well
operated fish stells end vegeteble nerkets, dock arees, a lower death rate
and cultural developments nark good municipal governnent. More then the
mere passage of levs or by-laws hes to be acconplished regulerly to meintain
schools, prevent fires when possible enl fight then when they occur, to ward
off epidenics, to lower a death or diseese rate or to provide a pure water

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.98)
4
supply. All such accomplishments are the produpts of skillful administra-
tion over a period of years. These great services are made pozsible chiefly
by the efficiency end loyelty of tpained techniciens. They depend elso in
part upon the extent of intelligent public cooperation, Indeed, civic minded-
ness is the great life-giving bese to all representative goverpment.
Municipal Policy end the Adminiotretion
The duty or obligation of the city edministretion is to execute end to
give real life to the expressed public policy of municipal effeirs. Whet is
meant by public municipel policy? How is it forned? Through whet steps do
the elected representatives go to produce this public polioy into final work-
eble form? How do the civic minded people end texpeyers see the results for
which they voted?
Simply put and of cardinal importance as the foundetion to representative
government, locel governmental policy or netional governmentel policy, is the
voters' and taxpeyers' expressed wishes and mendates to eccomplish a giyen
objective or goal. Public policy is settled at the ballot box with a ballot -
not with an assissan's bullet or by "a glorious dictator." To everyone it
must be clear that public policy is the citizens' ideas of what they believe
should be done. Their payment of taxes or their raising of special funds is
the means by which whatever they went done, can be done. It is also clear
that public policy begins and is generated by the people. Municipel policy
or nationel policy in representative government is never the conjured dictates
of sone one man, never the wilful arbitrary action of one or two men coupled
with a small "wilful group of little men." Never for certain is it one or a
series of cunning ects of a party of graeping rogues, labeling themselves
to disguise or to mystify busy and unsuspecting voters and taxpayers with

Specific Services of the Aaninistration

Structural Factors of Adninistration
When the elected representetives of the city governrient decide how many
functions and services and which ones will be performed, the problem arisee
autonatically as to the nanner and neans by which they can be carried out.
There must be some prectical end economicel arrangenent of the administra-
tion's offices and divisions. Thare must be some logic and a purpose to the
arrangement. The arrangenent is the mechanicel elenent of the adninistretion.
While the arrangenent is nechenical, its planning cennot be passed over light-
ly. Its structure requires that continuing and the nost careful thought be
put upon it.
The greatest nunber of municipal administrations in all the western
countries shows the arrengement to be on a functional basis. The effort is
constantly made to keep the performence of the function within the specific
office or division determined for it. This is not en easy matter to keep
under control. In the execution of a given function, other functions emerge
fron it and still other functions are related to it.
Without cere end pre-
cision in arrengerent, overlepping functions develop. They are always cost-
ly; they are for most of the personnel the source of confusion and jealousy.
Examples of a function with such offshoots of other end related funations
can be found in: (1) the finance departnent which if set up as e complete
function could include (a) taxetion, (b) other revenue producers, (c) bonds,
(d) the budget, (e) purchasing, (f) disbursement, (g) insurence, (h) auditing;
and (2) a public safety departnent which could inelude (a) police, (b) fire.
and (c) sanitation. It is obvious in both of these examples that while the
sub-divisions ere functionally related, at the sane tine some are function-
ally different enough to be singled out. They should be adninistered in par-
ticuler divisions end channels of their own. In Australia end in neny cities
in the United States the function of euditing is separated fron the general
administration.
Following the decision thet the arrangement of services will be upon
functional lines, there is next the specific task of organizing not only the
total nunber of divisions or offices within the administration but elso the
total number of levels of puthority which will operate it.
The nathenatics
16

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
for arriving at the solution to the nunber of levels of authority p
to some bureaucrats' desires, need not involve georetric progressionm 
number of levels should be kept few and the channel of authority within ths
division, as well as fron the division to the Office of Mayor, should be
direct and clear. The divisions of the adninistration may be called bureaus
or departments; these may be divided into sections, these into sub-sections
and these further into branches. Thus it is that a hierarchy of authority
has been created naturally and eutonaticelly; the branch chiefs report to
the sub-section chief and the sub-section chiefs to the section chief. and
these finally report to the department chief. The department chiefs. as the
highest in the adninistration, constitute the meyor's first level of execu-.....
tive subordinates; hence, they consult with end take direction personally
fron the mayor. The hierarchy of authority must be friendly and clear to
ell, not aloof and nystical.
A fourth end final decision to be nade by the city governnent in deter-
nining the structure of the administration concerns the nunber of officials
to control the departrent or office. Will the number be just one person or
several persons?
In addition to the four reasons given in Paper No. 5 of
this series for several persons by the establishnent of conmittees, conmis-
sions and boards to adninister certain functions insteed of just one person.
there is another significant reason which cones directly fron a principle
of public administration end personnel managenent. The principle is stated:
When the function to be administered is of a quasi-judicial or deliberative
cheracter, divided responsibility or group decision cen best be utilized.
Sone functions by their very nature can be executed in a more practical and
useful nanner by a plural suthority. Orders of the office are arrived at
more judiciously by e plurel euthority than by an egency of one person. Ad-
ministrators and officiels with long public experience state that for such
a particuler function and for the decisions naking the orders to execute it
"the balanced judenent of three or nore qualified persons" must be used.
This principle can re stated fron the opposite point of view: When
the function to be exocated is pirely adninistrative - that is, quite opera-
tional in character - or at l ast nore adninistrative than deliberative in
nature, undivided responsibility or single conrand or a single body can best

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
be utilized. This neans the t the poliey or the decision making and the es
sential course for action have been deternined somewhat in advance. Also it
means that the execution is charactorized by straight supervision or direc-
tion. At the same time, however, this does not mean that the single conmand
does not have the power of discretion to use as he administers the function.
Thus it is that decisions for. the order to execute a non-deliberative func-
tion are arrived at more judiciously by one person rather than three or more.
Arising from this comes the modern doctrine of individual responsibility
of a public servant for his own executive actions. In most countries this
is in force only on the higher levels but the idea is gradually being applied
lower down. Institutes of public administration have done much to propagate
this.
Municipal Organization in Japan
The executive authority of the city in Jepan either supervises or co-
ordinates all the offices of the administration, the independent committees,
commissions and boards and Office of the City Treasurer. In order to observe
how the city "keeps house" for the citizenry, the executive branch will be
shown and discussed in its three essential parts.
1.
The Office of Mayor. The major functions of this Office are given in
Paper No. 3 of this series. Discussion here is ontirely on the coordinetive
and supervisory manner in which the mayor directs the city administration.
The mayor of the city must devote his full time to policy issues: (1)
the overall policy for the municipality es determined by the electorate at
the ballot box, (2) the divisions of thet policy as expressed legally in
jorei and resolutions, and (3) the implementing policy and plans which the
mayor himself establishes to execute the jurei and resolutions. His imple-
menting plans and policies must be goered within the city's overall policy
and the specific requircments of the laws and jurei.
Therefore, the carry-
ing of this policy respcnsibility is difficult and it requires long, busy
hours outside the business day. On specific occasions, results caused by
the execution of particular policies will develop in the city that will make
the mayor alter his implementing plens end take a new course of action.
The
mayor through prompt edvice from his executive subordinates will know how
his plans are working out. Thus the mayor can foresee the development of

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.97)
ped-or poor results. He will, of course, chenge his course of ection to
prevent the errors from occurring. To be eble to do this amoothly requitom
a flexible policy with which to stert.
The sefer principle elweye
which the Office of Mayor should be guided is: "Eveluate conditions care
fully beforehend and thus prevent improvidence." The other principle.
"Eliminete all those who oppose, " has certein merits to it but, even with
these, the principle is limited. Should the meyor practice this principle
too freely, he will find that the number of those whom he has eliminated
will soon grow in sufficiency so that he himself will be eliminated!
Certain euthority for the disposition of particuler affeirs should be
lodged with the assistant meyors. The competency of the assistent mayors
can best be mede clear to sll by heving it set forward in the city's by-laws.
The degree to which end the affeirs in which the assistant meyors assume
the actual powers of the meyor, ought to be defined end enumerated in writing.
Some cities have accomplished this necessity elready. In other cities the
mayors on this matter heve acted only edministratively within their office.
Although this latter practice is not as finished a piece of executive work
es the former, it is better then the prectice yet found in which only oral
delegations end instructions oxist between the mryor end the assistent mayors.
Article 152 and Article 153 of the Chihojichiho provide the authority for
the city government to define the assistent mayor's duties. Articles 122.
127, 130 end 133 of the Cebinet Order also relate to the assistent nayors
assuming the meyor's duties.
The essistent meyors should make the decisions on the defined delegations
and be held responsible for them. The duty of such important officers is to
assume most of the executive responsibilities for the finel action on the
city governnent's affairs. Assistant meyors cennot be mere executive ad-
visors; they must be evaluators and seloctors in exercising their executive
authority. Their duty is to select and to give only the more important papers
to the mayor for his personal attention and decision.
Moreover, when the assis'ent mayors make their decisions, the nayor should
give them his support.
ee is the ebsolute presumption that when the mayor

Saving the Texpeyers! Money
The cry to save city epenses is chronic. Seemingly individuels and
all parties takc up the slogen, "Seve the taxpeyers' noney!"
The voters heve
to hear this megic phrase over end over eain.
Some people mean what they

There

9

Too meny people with savings of expenses in mind approach the governmentel

Therefore, they force the principle of "pinch penny finence" into operation

The following illustrations show how study end investigation cen produce
savings in money and manpower:
1. A city police department meintained an office for emergency services
for ell types of eccidents: automobiles, trains, gas leaks, asphyxia, fires,
and for assistance to police authorities in difficulties. The personnel
strength was epproximately 500 policemen and the office had 20 emergency
trucks. The fire depertnent mainteined a staff of l00 firemen and 4 rescue
trucks to do almost precisely the sane duties. By consolideting the function
in the fire department, (1) no additionel men hed to be added to the fire de-
partment, (2) more then 350 policemen were freed for reguler police duty,
(3) three-fourths of the trucks were trensferred from the police department,
end (4) over one million dollers e year in salery end police service time were
seved.
2. A city had three very lerge departnents, which over a period of years

medical staffs end laboratcries. T.  personnel of all three departments

meterially from one depertment service to the other, end some subordinate
medical doctors actually received more money then their supervisors. (2) the

policy, and (4) sick leave pcliy end ccmpeneation payments to employees

3. In a city of relatively small size, eech of five departments main-

4. In many large cities in the United Stetes, hand labor when used

5. Some cities have found that the equipment which they possess is not

The orders and decisions of the guasi-legislativs, quasi-jud

The eggrieved pariy comes to court complaining that the conmittee's,
conmission's or board!s orders or rulings ere oppressive and unreasonable.
These two terms meen thet the plaintiff feels the rulings to be arbitrary,
capricious or outright confiscatory of his or her rights.
The court's furctions ere (1) to determine whether the commission has
statutory authority to act, whether its acts were reasonable, and then. (2)
to tell the parties what they must do, either plaintiff or defendent.
When the orgenic act setting up the egency is well drefted and clear, the
judge has little difficuity in deciding the competency of the board. The
judge may then decide whether the boerd's action was reesonable. He does
so by finding out (1) whether or not the action conforms to the law, (2)
whether or not the action wes, before being teken, besed upon competent
evidence or findings, and (3) whether or not it was issued arbitrarily.
Thus it is that the decisions of committees, boerds end commissions
are subject to judiciel review in the courts of Jepan. Not only can their
decisions be reviewed but in certein cases they cen be restrained. The
agencies can be enjoined by injunctive relief from having their orders

The Public Safety Comnission
The Police Law in Chepter III, Section 1, Article 40, places cleerly
the authority and power of law enforcement and policing in every city of
Japan and certain other autonomous entities: "Cities and urban communi-

towns and villages) shall be resporsible for the naintenance of police
end enforcement of law end order within their boundaries."
In the sane clear manner, Article 43 pleces the control of the police
authorities in the Public Safety Commission: "There shall be established.
under the jurisdiction of the mayors of cities end headnen of towns and
villages Public Safety Connissions of cities, towns and villages to control
the police within the boundaries of the respective cities, towns and vil-
lages."
It is absolutely fundamental to the understending and solving of law
enforcenent problens in Japan that Article 54 of the Police Law be kept
in mind: "There shall exist neither administrative nor operational control
by the Netional Rural Police over the police of cities, towns end villages.
These police shall be obligated to cocperate with each other."
Thus it is that the purposes of this nunicipal systen ere (1) to place
the responsibility of policing in local jurisdiutions; (2) to repose in the
local conmunities one of the nost impoe tent govonmental functions; and (3)
to have the Public Sefety Connission control the police within the boundaries
of the city.

Summery:
Three Conelusions
The power and suthority of ell the local governments in Japen to es-
tablish independent egencies to perforn e perticular function should end
must be used with intelligence end real noderation. Good causes end good
reasons ought to be in evidence before connittees, commissions or boerds
are established. These egencies need not be esteblished simply to nake.
roon for sone person's forty.fifth cousin, especielly when the other forty-
four cousins ere already having lunch off the municipelity's unknowing

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)
taxpayersl Indeed, these egencies need not be eetalliahed aat
Monopoly or the Friends' Institution! A nunicipal government 
ment can easily be harpered or its euthority dissipated wilaly by amalsi-
plicity of offices end agencies. Friction dovelops, diecontent matures
and inter-deparinental jealousies becone rife; good governnent is vitiated
quickly and easily by overdoing the egencies. Moderation in nuiber or
function of agencies will produce no public censure or bad politicel re-
percussions.
When connittees, conmissions or boards ere created, their function
most assuredly is to perform the given task, to do the work; in short, they
are duty bound to produce: Who is to see that the agencies do produce?
How is it done? Does the checker or inspector close his eyes, squint with
one, or open both? Sone agencies report to the public, some to the mayor,
some to the assembly and sone to edministrative offices. Each of these
parties to whon the agency is responsible has the duty to check and to
evaluate results. If an egency does not or cannot produce, even after
change of personnel, the nayor nust abolish it or the essenbly legislate
it off the books. The keeping of useless agencies is just grounds for
wholesale criticism of tho city's governnent.
What should the nayor, the particular cifice, the assembly or the
city governnent as a whole do with the cornittee's, the connission's or
board's technical advice or professional recommendetion? Throw it away?
Use it to start an argument? Or file it safely in sone little dark corner
where none need see it? When an agency of technicelly experienced or pro-
fessionally trained persormel presents its findings and recormendations,
the public, the asserbly, the mayor or the adninistrative office - each is
duty obligated to reccive, to anelyze and to give the most careful atten-
tion. To go counter to a qualified agency's report, the burden is on the
perty doing so to produce the strongest evidence. Why have the agency
otherwise? Why use the texpeyerst money? Indeed, Honeori zon no katabire
moke: To lebor hard but to gain nothing but fatigue.

出されるもの為言す。

Subject: Opening of Sixth Special Diet Session.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
ZXR's
Sir:
I have the honor to bring to the Department's
attention some of the political developments which have
arisen in connection with the opening of the sixth
special session of the Japanese National Diet.
The two houses of the Diet convened on October 25
with the Government of Prime Minister YOSHIDA Shigeru
indicating its intention to introduce forty-three
bills, several of which are concerned with supplementary
revisions of the 1949-1950 budget in order to meet the
tox reforms recom  ded
the Shoup Mission. It is
oxpected that the coming session will test the Yoshida
Cabinet's ability to effect its long-term economic
stabilization program
The Prime Minister's administrative
speech is scheduled to be delivered on Novemb  8.
Economic problems will unquestionably dominate
the sixth special session In addition to implementing
the tax reduction reforms recom ended by the Shoup Mission,
the government will present a number of important bills
to supplement the measures concerned with the Nine-Point
Economic Stabilization Program enacted at the fifth special
session. It is expected that the pending visit of
r. Joseph M. DODGE, financial adviser to SCAP,at the
end of October will delay deliberations on economic legisla-
tion. The special session, however, is presently scheduled
to close before the end of November.
From the partisan standpoint it is not expected that
the Yoshida Cabinet will experience much difficulty in
FILED
guiding its legislative program through the Diet. In the
C
House

House of Representatives the Democratic-Liberal Party
holds 266 out of the House's 466 seats. Additionally,
the government party can count to some extent upon the.
support of 33 coalition Democrats. The prinoipal opposi-
tion will come from the Social Democratic Party (which
however, holds only 48 seats) and the 37 opposition
Democrats. A noisy Communist contingent of 36 members
will, of course, oppose practically every government
measure, but it is doubtful if the Communists can
effectively collaborate with the other opposition parties.
XR
The remaining seats of the House of Representatives
consist of the New Political Council, 32, the Labor-
894.00B
Farmer Party, 6, and the Kosei Club, 4, with 1 Independent
and 3 vacancies.
While the Democratic-Liberal Party thus dominates
the House of Representatives, the Yoshida Cabinet does
not enjoy such an advantageous position in the House of
Councillors. Of the 250 seats, the Democratio Liberals
hold only 52 and will, accordingly, have to depend upon
support from the 75 Ryokufukai members. The Democrats
and Socialists each hold 42 seats, with 16 seats for the
Mushozoku Kodankai (an affiliation of Independents),
7 f. the Shinsei Club, 6 for the Communists, 3 Independents,
and 7 vacancies. Councillors holding three-year terms.
XR
come up for election next year and it is expected that.
many of them will seek the limelight for campaign purposes
894.002
by loud ettack against the government.
In a statement to. the press on October 22, Chief
Cabinet Secretary MASUDA Kaneshichi gave some indication
of the character of the Government's legislative program.
Looming large in the budget for the 1950-5l fiscal year
are appropriations for relief funds under a public works
program: thirty billion yen for emergency relief, twenty-
four billion for permanent relief, and ten billion as a
reserve The Government will endeavor, according to
Mr asuda. to set the new base wage at ¥6, 3o7, but it
will not be possible to revise the base wage for public
servants at the present session. An 80 percent increase
in railway freight rates is contemplated in an attempt
to adjust those rates with the increased passenger fares
enacted at the last session. While some consideration
is being given to increasing rates for electricity, no
definite decision on this question has yet been reached.
It is possible that t he opposition in both houses
of the Diet will center its attacks more on what the
Govern ent does not propose to undertake than upon the
legislative program the Government introduces. It is
unlikely, for example, that the Yoshida Cabinet will
attempt to revive the year-end bonus system for government
personnel
RESTRICTED

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch
no: 745, October 27
1949。
RESTRICTED
-3-
personnel, an issue which is certain to arouse the
opposition. There is also considerable demand for a
revision of the foreign exchange rate of 360 yen to
one U. S. dollar, and strong opposition to the present
deflationary policies which have brought about a tight
money situation. These are problems which it is not
expected that the Yoshida Cabinet will. or can deal with
directly in its new legislative program, and failure to
do so will likely provide the opposition with effective
issues to criticize the Cabinet. Since many of these
issues have a direct bearing on the labor situation,
opposition leaders will most probably endeavor to channel
discussion and debate, especially in the House of Repre-
sentatives, to questions which arouse such popular
interest.
It is also expected that some of the opposition parties
will attempt to bring up the question of a peace conference
at the present session, primarily as a deviceto embarrass
the Yoshida Cabinet.
While the Democratic-Liberal Party enjoys a commanding
position in the House of Representatives and it is the
general belief that the Yoshida Cabinet's legislative
program can be successfully carried through the speoial
session, there are indications that the center parties
may hereafter present a better organized and more effective
opposition. The most conspicuous political feature of the
fifth special session of the Diet which met. from February 11
to May 31, 1949, was the so-called polarization of Japanese
parliamentary politics as a result of the general election
in January, In the fifth session the majority Demoeratic-
Liberal Party was primarily pitted against an ineffective
but noisy Communist minority with the other parties of
the center in a state of confusion and disorganization as
a result of internal strife.
As the present sixth special session convenes,
however, the polarized character of the Diet is much
less evident, The Democratic Party has virtually split
into two parties. One, the so-called coalition faction
which is of conservative temperament, appears prepared to
work closely with the majority Democratic-Liberal Party.
The other faction, the anti-coalitionists, will probably
not cooperate with the Socialists, but on October 24 this
group entered into a working agreement with the New
Political Council for the purpose of waging a joint fight
in the Diet on such problems as the rice coupon system,
the proposed transfer of the government tobacco monopoly
system to private industry, and the revision of legislation
concerning the distribution and sale of foodstuffs. This
may, of course, lead to an all-round coalition between
these two opposition parties.
At the
RESTRICTED

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch
no。 745, 0ctober 27,
1949。
RESTRICTED
At the same time, the Social Democratic Party has
begun to show indications of some rejuvenation whioh
may make it a more effective opposition party with or
without cooperation from the anti-coalition Demoorats
the Communists, or any of the other minor groups. At
its National Congress in March 1949, the Social Democratic
Party attempted a far-reaching reorganization whioh may
result, in the coming session, in a more aggressive type
of opposition than the moribund appearance the party
presented in the last session (this Mission's despatch
no. 242 of April 21, 1949). Under the leftist leadership
of Mr, SUZukI Losaburo and as the result of the infiltration
o. Democratization League Members into the party, some
of whom are former Communists and have all the Communists'
ability for rough-and-tumble politics, the Socialists
have begun to assume a new character. The program the
party has announced for the sixth special session calls
for the introduction of a vote of non-confidence in the
Yoshida Cabinet and the adoption of typically communist
"struggle" policies, which will include an effort to
"seek a concentration of forces outside the Diet with.
the labor-farmer front as their axis". In other words
according to a policy program adopted by Socialist
leaders at a meeting on October 24, the Social Democratic
Party will take a leaf from the Communists' book and
attempt to organize a farmer-labor offensive to parallel
its anti Yoshida activities in the Diet. How far the
party intends to implement this offensive with strikes and
labor agitation is not clear, but it is unlikely that
the party. even with the assistance of its Democratization
League foilowers, has yet been able to develop "struggle"
techniques which would produce the type of labor offensives
leunched by the Communists in the last three years. Never-
theless, these developments at least indicate the intention
of the Social Democratic Party to play a more aggressive
role in and outside the Diet, using non-communist segments.
of the labor movement as the basis for its activity.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

GENLRAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPRIE COIELANDER FOR THE ALLÉED POUERS
Civil Infomation and Education*Section
Public Opinion and Sociolocical Research Division
3 November 1949.
Data From
A RECENT SURVEY OF POLITICAL TRINDS
On 11-12 October 1949, the Asahi Newspaper Public Opinion Room con-
ducted a nation-wide survey of current political attitudes. In the course
of this survey, a sample of over 3000ipersons, selected by systematic
random methods from election rosters, was intervieved.

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despaton
1949, entitled
Towards Japan,"
OFFICE OF THE PRESIDENT OF THE PHILIPPINES
OFFICE OF PUBLIC INFORMATION
Malacañan, Manila
PRESS RELEASE NO. 10-17-2
October 17, 1949
SUBJECT: SEC. NERI'S RADIO TALK ON
7:20 P.M.
"CABINET REPORTS SERIES"
SHEET 2.
LADIES AND GENTLEMEN:
During the last four weeks or so, events of far-reaching conse-
quence have occurred which have radically altered the political and
economic climate of the post-war world. In the Philippines, these
developments have registered with telling effect. Press dispatches
from London, Moscow, Peiping and Washington have bewildered the man
on the street. He began to ask a few pointed questions which demand
straightforward answers.
It might, therefore, be worth our while to recall a few of these
events.
On September 19 an announcement from London told the world that
Great Britain had devalued the pound. On September 23, President Tru-
man made the historic announcement in Washington that Russia had ex-
ploded her first atomic bomb, thus ending the American monopoly of this
dreaded weapon. On October 1, Red leader Mao Tze-Tung proclaimed in
Peiping the establishment of the People's Republic of China and simul-
taneously made a bid for international recognition of the new Chinese
regime.
I cite these three world-shaking events not merely because they
will certainly influence the future trend of international diplomacy
but also because they are typical examples of the world-forces which
affect the developmental pattern of the Republic's foreign policy.
Let me illustrate how this series of events exert their influence
on the changing pattern of our foreign policy, the task of charting
which is the primary responsibility of the Department of Foreign Af.
fairs. When Great Britain devalued the pound, policy-makers in the
Philippines re-evaluated the position of the young Republic within the
context of Britain's currency devaluation and its probable effects on
our economy. There were strong and basic differences of opinion in the
ensuing discussion on the subject. However, despite the resulting at-
mosphere of spirited controversy the participants in this discussion
(more)

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
shared a marked identity of views on the "incalculable" effecte
ther good or bad, that the devaluation would have on Philippine
I have often been confronted by questions like, "What is the
reign policy of the Philippines?" or "How would you define a nati
foreign policy?" Frankly, these questions beggar any categorical
answer. It is next to impossible to define exactly what the Repub-
lic's foreign policy is nor to delimit the scope of that policy. It
would be much easier, however, to understand the character and pat-
tern of that foreign policy by evaluating it against a broad outline
of its cardinal objectives.
Within the brief life of the Republic, the Philippines has evol-
ved the following salient objectives of her foreign policy:
1. The promotion and maintenance of friendly relations with all
nations of the world;
2. Adherence to the principles of the United Nations Charter and
support of the measures takcn by that international organization to
maintain international pecce and security, solve pressing economic,
social and cultur al problems of international importance and promote
fundamental human rights for all;
3. Promotin of the mutually advantageous special relatin s bet-
ween the Philippines and the United Statcs;
4. Adherence to the principles of democracy and the preservation
of our d emocratic way of life;
5. Support of the asrirations of dependent and non-self-governing
peoples to solf-determination;
6. Early and mater ial recognition of our just claims against Ja-
pan and an unequivocal position against Japan's resurgence in the
future as a dominant pover capable once more of threatening the peace
of this part of the world; and
7. Development of the Republic's national economy along lines of
international cooperation and regional integration.
In the implementation of the first objective of our foreign policy,
the Republic is cnergetically lining up new friends all over the world
and in turn is the object of gestur es of f riendship f rom countrie s big
and small. Evidence of this is t he conglomerate of bilateral treaties
and pacts concluded by t he Republic and the multila teral conventin s
(more)

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
SHEET
and protocols to which the Republic is a signatory. Altogether,
the Philippines has concluded 70 different treaties with various
countries all over the world while 20 pacts of differing character
are now under negotiation. Of these treaties, 8 are pacts of amity
with the United States, China, France, Italy, Spain, Ecuador, Turkey
and Thailand, while similar pacts are currently under negotiation
with 6 countries, namely: India, Brazil, Greece, Mexico, Pakistan
and Burma. We are at present conducting negotiations for the purpose
of concluding treaties of commerce and navigation with the United
States, Argentina, Italy, China and Egypt. In addition, the Philip-
pines has already signed 8 air transport agreements with 8 different
countries. Nine similar agreements are scheduled for early signature.
The Philippines is furthermore a signatory to 7 conventions and pro-
tocols with humanitarian objectives and 5 conventions and protocols
on communications.
Proof of the Republic's zealous bid for the friendship of other
countries is the number of iplomatic and consular offices that the
Philippines has been able to establish all over the world despite
budgetary limitations. Today, the Republic maintains an embassy in
Washington; two special missions consisting of the Philippine mission
in the Unite? Nations an? the special mission to SCaP in Tokyo; 6
legations, namely in Lon on, Buenos Aires, Rome, Bangkok, Madrid and
Nanking; 6 consulates general in San Francisco, Honolulu, New York,
Sydney, Calcutta, and Shanghai; 8 consulates including an honorary
consular office in Guam.
It may be pertinent to mention that the Philippines has given
importance to the establishment of new consular offices in Asia and
the Far East. For this renson, e consulate general has been recently
opened in Calcutta and consulates est blished in Karachi, and in
Batavia esp&te the inadequacy of the Department's appropriations in
this year's budget.
Plans are unler consideration to complete this
network of consular posts in Southeast Asia by the establishment of
offices in Singapore an Rangoon.
(more)

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
SHEET 5.
Most of these consular establishments have since become se
supporting although, by their very nature, they are not supposed to
be revenue-producing offices. For every peso invested by our govern-
ment in their maintenance, we have earned back 40 cents in consular
fees.
The opening of these new offices reflects the increasing pre-
occupation of the Repuhlic with the affairs of her neighbors and sig-
nificantly underscores our esire not only to stabilize our relations
with these neighbors but to integrate our infant economy with the
overall regional economy
of Asia and the Far East.
The Philippines has faith in the principles of the United Na-
tions Charter. That faith has found
eloquent expression in the ac-
complishments of
our delegations to that international organization,
its organs and specialized agencies, particularly our delegation to
the Generel Assembly under the able leadership of Ambassador Carlos
P. Romulo.
Ambassador Romulo, in his brief speech before the General
Assembly shortly after his election as president echoed that faith
which is now manifest in his current efforts to bring about an early
settlement of the United States-Soviet impasse on the control of
atomic emergy.
The four years of Philippine participation in the Councils of the
United Nations is a proud record of initiative if not leadership in
the field of international cooperation. A recapitulation of the long
list of Philippine contributions in the shaping of vital policy deci-
sions adopted at the meetings of the United Nations' specialized agen-
cies such as the ECAFE, FAO, UNESCO, ILO, WHO, ICAO, the World Bank,
non-United Nati ns agencies such as the now defunct IEFC in Washing-
ton would be superfluous. They are a matter of common knowledge.
In this connection, I shoula like to echo President Quirino's
reiteration of the fact that our initiative in the formation of the
projected Southeast Asia Union, was not, as a few originally suspected,
designed to make the Union operate outsile of but within the framework
of the United Nations.
(more)

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
SHEET 6.
The mutually advantageous relationship that exists at presen
between the Philippines and the United States has a background of
years of fruitful association between the two countries.
It is pre
cisely the existence of these
special ties that sometimes exposes
Philippine foreign policy to the criticism that our foreign policy
follows the pattern set in Washington. This criticism has no founda-
tion in fact.
Philippine foreign policy has not been dependent upon that of the
United States. The sharply livergent lines occasionally pursued by
the two governments on a broad field of issues clearly prove this
assertion. I cite our position as regards Franco Spain and Japan on
which the Philippines presented different views.
Staunch, unswerving support of the aspirations of non-self-
governing peoples towards independence has become a moral commitment
of our Republic in the field of international diplomacy. As the first
nation in this part of the world to attain independent status since
the War, the Philippines stands out as an example of a nation which
attained freedom through peaceful and progressive evolution.
This as-
pect of the foreign policy structure of the Republic is easily mani-
fest in the
Philippines' espousal of the
cause of
Indonesia in the
councils of the United Nations. Recently, we approved a credit line
for the Republic of Indonesia to enable her to hasten her rehabilita-
tion and we voted for her admission as
an associate member of the
ECAFE.
Only the other day at the United Nations trusteeship committee
meeting in Lake Success the Philippine resolution binding "governments
which have colonial responsibilities" to immediately set programs lead-
ing to the "ultimete independence of territories under trusteeship
control" was approved.
This sympathy for the so-called "unredeemed minorities" also ex-
plains the Philippine position on the problem of the disposition of the
former Italian colonies of Libya, Somaliland and Eritrea. This posi-
tion may be described as
one
of strict observance of the fundamental
wishes of the peoples of
the
areas
concerned.
The same ardent sympathy
for the cause of colonial peoples is evident in the Philippine stand
(more)
